# records/research-pipeline.md — the timeline's research record

GENERATED by `tools/timeline-pipeline-record.mjs` from the research workflows' run journals.
Not written from memory: every verdict below is what a verifier agent actually returned.

Blueprint §4.4 and T-13. Every Source id that ships appears here with the verdict of an
adversarial verifier that **re-fetched the URL** and checked the excerpt against the live page.
Nothing entered the pool on an author's word alone.

## The method

For each batch, in order:

1. **Author** — a fetch-capable agent with the schema, the walls, and a target milestone list.
   It searched, fetched, and recorded a verbatim excerpt of at most twenty-five words containing
   the figure. It was told explicitly that an honest `researchRequired` beats a plausible
   invention, and the later batches were given the specific traps the earlier verifiers had
   caught.
2. **Adversarially verify** — a SEPARATE agent, given the written batch file but never the
   author's reasoning, instructed to assume the batch is wrong. It re-fetched every URL and
   checked: excerpt verbatim on the page; at most twenty-five words; contains the figure; every
   figure in `timing` present in an excerpt of that record's own sources; claim does not exceed
   source; state- and condition-varying rules not collapsed to one age; normative language;
   digits in prose; crisis tier; loss tier; sensitivity flags and required routes; reader
   address; percentages on a surface; and `typical` contained by `window`.
3. **Repair** — every BLOCKING finding fixed before the batch was listed, either by fetching a
   source that does state the figure, or by demoting the record to the research-required shape.

**A caveat stated rather than hidden:** the fetch tool caches a URL's content for about fifteen
minutes, so a verifier re-fetching soon after its author may have read a cached copy of the same
page. The check that the excerpt IS on the page is still real; what is weaker than it looks is
the claim of an independent network round trip. The executor's own twenty-source re-fetch
(`records/invent-nothing-test.md`) was run later, separately, and is the stronger evidence.

  > I re-fetched all 5 unique URLs behind the 6 source records (src-p1fc-usc-23-158-age and -withholding share one govinfo URL; both excerpts were checked in that fetch). Nothing 404'd, redirected, or was paywalled. All 6 excerpts are verbatim contiguous on-page strings, all are at or under 25 words, and every timing figure (21, 21, 26, 24) does appear in an excerpt belonging to that record's own sources — no invented numbers. Publisher/publicationYear/dataYear check out on four of six (CDC page published 2024-05-15; FDA "content current as of 08/07/2025"; both govinfo sections are the 2023 edition). No digits in any prose field, no normative-language hits, no crisis- or loss-tier vocabulary, no reader address in record prose, no recommended timing, no numeric record without sources, and both readRefs (/topics/health, /map/credential-decision) are on the allowed list.
  > 
  > Two blocking findings. The serious one is the ACA milestone: the healthcare.gov page the record cites says in its own words that "Some states and plans have different rules" and directs readers to their state Department of Insurance, and its 26 sentence is hedged with "usually" — yet the record encodes exact: 26 and asserts that dependent coverage "stops being an option." That is a state-varying rule collapsed into an exact age, and it exceeds the cited source, contradicted by the source itself. The second is the §158 age excerpt, which is verbatim but was trimmed mid-clause so that the displayed string reads "...by a person who is less than twenty-one years of age is lawful" — standing alone it asserts the opposite of the milestone it supports.
  > 
  > Five advisory findings follow, the notable one being the alcohol record's unsourced "every state" / "public possession" generalization.

- **BLOCKING findings (2) — all repaired before listing:**
    - `ms-parent-health-plan-dependent-coverage-ends` — A state- and plan-varying rule is collapsed into timing.exact = 26, and whatChanges[0] states the outcome absolutely ("Staying on a parent's plan as a dependent stops being an option"). The cited page itself contradicts both. Rule C violation, compounded by rule B (claim exceeds source): the source's own sentence is hedged ("you USUALLY can be added ... and stay on it until you turn 26"), and the same page carries explicit variation language the record drops.
        - found on re-fetch: Re-fetched https://www.healthcare.gov/young-adults/children-under-26/. The excerpt is verbatim and present under the heading "How to get or stay on a parent's plan." But the same page also reads: "Check with the employer or plan if you can stay on the plan after you turn 26. Some states and plans have different rules." and "Contact your state's Department of Insurance for state-specific rules." The page further lists circumstances in which dependents remain on job-based plans despite turning 26. The record cites this page as its only source and reports none of that.
        - fix: Either (a) change timing from exact: 26 to a bounded/typical form that carries the federal floor plus state and plan variation, and rewrite whatChanges[0] so it describes the federal requirement ending rather than coverage ending — e.g. the plan's obligation to offer dependent coverage under the federal rule stops here, while some state rules and some plans continue it; or (b) keep exact: 26 but narrow the label, population and whatChanges to the federal minimum requirement only, and add a second whatChanges line stating that state rules and individual plans vary. Also add a source that carries the federal requirement directly (45 CFR 147.120 or the CMS rule page) so the exact figure rests on the regulation rather than on a consumer page that hedges it.
    - `src-p1fc-usc-23-158-age` — The excerpt is verbatim but was cut mid-clause at both ends, and the fragment that results reverses the statute's meaning. Displayed on its own as the evidence under a milestone titled "Legal age to buy alcohol" with exact: 21, the string reads as an assertion that purchase by a person under twenty-one is lawful. An evidence string that states the opposite of the record it supports is not usable as displayed evidence.
        - found on re-fetch: Re-fetched https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USCODE-2023-title23/html/USCODE-2023-title23-chap1-sec158.htm (2023 edition confirmed). The excerpt appears verbatim, but only as the tail of this sentence: "The Secretary shall withhold 10 per centum of the amount required to be apportioned to any State ... in which the purchase or public possession in such State of any alcoholic beverage by a person who is less than twenty-one years of age is lawful." The clause is the CONDITION FOR WITHHOLDING FUNDS. Stripped of "The Secretary shall withhold ... in which the purchase or public possession in such State," the remainder inverts.
        - fix: Re-cut the excerpt so the trimmed string carries the withholding sense within the 25-word cap — e.g. "withhold 10 per centum of the amount required to be apportioned to any State ... in which the purchase or public possession" is still awkward, so prefer a span that keeps subject and condition together, such as "The Secretary shall withhold 10 per centum of the amount required to be apportioned to any State" paired with the existing withholding excerpt, and let the CDC source carry the age. Alternatively drop this source record entirely: the milestone's figure is already carried by src-p1fc-cdc-mlda and the mechanism by src-p1fc-usc-23-158-withholding, so this excerpt adds risk without adding evidence.
- **Advisory findings (5):**
    - `ms-alcohol-purchase-minimum-age` — whatChanges[0] claims "the same threshold applies across the country because every state sets its purchase and public possession age at the federal mark." Neither the universal quantifier nor the public-possession half appears in any excerpt on this record, and the causal "because" asserts a mandate the cited statute does not create. Rule B (claim exceeds source), and the possession half brushes rule C — state law on under-21 possession and consumption carries widely varying exceptions (private residence, parental presence, religious use, employment) that this sentence flattens.
    - `src-p1fc-healthcare-under-26` — The excerpt addresses the reader in the second person twice ("you usually can be added", "until you turn 26"). Rule J bars sentences that speak to "you" about your own life, and rule J is written against "any sentence," not against record prose only. Excerpts are reader-facing strings. This creates a direct conflict with the verbatim requirement, since healthcare.gov is written in second person throughout — so the fix is a different source, not a reworded excerpt.
    - `src-p1fc-healthcare-under-26` — publicationYear and dataYear are both recorded as 2026, which is the retrieval year, not a publication year or a data year. The underlying rule has been in force since 2010. A downstream consumer reading dataYear 2026 will believe the figure was measured or the rule was set in 2026.
    - `ms-independent-status-federal-student-aid` — timing.exact = 24 does not match the statutory trigger. The condition is age 24 or older BY DECEMBER 31 OF THE AWARD YEAR, so an applicant who turns 24 at any point in that calendar year qualifies for the award year that begins while they are still 23. Encoding it as an exact age of 24 renders as "at 24" and slightly overstates the age at which the status actually attaches. Rule B, mild.
    - `ms-alcohol-purchase-minimum-age` — whatChanges[2] ("Stores, bars and restaurants ask for proof of age, so carrying identification becomes part of ordinary social occasions for many people.") is a behavioral claim about population conduct, and no source on this record touches it. Not a figure, so not rule A, but it is an unsourced empirical assertion in a record whose status is "researched".
- **Repair pass:** verdict `clean`.

  > Source substitutions you should know about:
  > - Student aid: studentaid.gov was unreachable — five separate paths (the dependency page, two help-center articles, the /resources text page, and two PDFs) each timed out at 60s. I sourced the age criterion from the primary statute instead: 20 U.S.C. 1087vv(d)(1)(A) on govinfo, verbatim "is 24 years of age or older by December 31 of the award year", confirmed present on the page by a second verification fetch. If the executor wants a studentaid.gov citation, that host needs a different fetcher.
  > - Alcohol: the NIAAA APIS page and the NHTSA fact sheet both returned 403, and eCFR part 1208 redirects away. I used 23 U.S.C. 158 on govinfo for both the age condition and the withholding mechanism (two Source records against the same URL, different excerpts), plus a CDC page for the numeral.
  > 
  > Deliberate excerpt choices, so the adversarial re-fetch is not surprised:
  > - Excerpts are contiguous verbatim substrings of on-page sentences, trimmed to fit the 25-word cap, not paraphrases and not stitched from separate places. Two were verified with a follow-up "does this exact string appear" fetch (both govinfo sections); the CDC and healthcare.gov strings were likewise confirmed present on a second fetch.
  > - The 23 U.S.C. 158 age excerpt spells the figure as "twenty-one", not "21", because that is how the statute reads. The numeral 21 for that milestone is carried by the CDC source ("The MLDA in the United States is 21 years."), which is listed first in that record's sources. A checker matching the literal string "21" must look at the CDC source, not the statute one.
  > - The alcohol record's second statute excerpt carries the figure 8 (percent of apportionment withheld for fiscal 2012 and after). That figure appears in no timing field and in no prose — it exists only to substantiate the "withholds a share of highway funding" line in whatChanges, which is written without digits.
  > 
  > Metadata assumptions I had to make:
  > - healthcare.gov/young-adults/children-under-26/ shows no publication or last-updated date. I recorded publicationYear and dataYear as 2026 (the retrieval year) and said so explicitly in that source's "measures" field rather than guessing at an earlier date.
  > - The FDA Tobacco 21 page states content current as of 08/07/2025, so publicationYear/dataYear 2025. The two govinfo sections are the 2023 edition of the U.S. Code, so 2023/2023.
  > 
  > Editorial calls that could reasonably go the other way:
  > - Alcohol and tobacco are both filed under lane "civic-legal" (the brief allowed body-health or civic-legal) with readRef /topics/health. If the build prefers those two in body-health, it is a one-word change per record.
  > - All four milestones are marked optional: true. Never buying alcohol or tobacco is plainly an ordinary path; never being on a parent's plan and never applying for federal student aid are also ordinary, so none of these is framed as something everyone passes through.
  > - The third whatChanges line on the ACA record ("Many people reach this point already covered elsewhere...") is an editorial framing line, not a sourced claim. It carries no figure. Cut it if the compiler treats every whatChanges line as needing source backing.
  > - Verified mechanically before finishing: valid JSON, no digits in any label/population/whatChanges field, every whatChanges line under 40 words, every excerpt at or under 25 words, every timing figure (21, 21, 26, 24) present in an excerpt of one of that record's own sources. No loss-tier or crisis-tier vocabulary appears anywhere, including in the excerpts — I avoided the CDC sentences about mortality for that reason.

The author's 59½ glyph concern is real but harmless — tools/timeline-build-content.mjs has NO figure-containment check at all, so nothing downstream does the literal "59.5" substring match they feared; I confirmed "59½" on both irs.gov pages myself. ONE BLOCKING DEFECT: the RMD record collapses a condition-varying federal rule into a single exact age. Its population explicitly includes "workplace retirement plan accounts", but the very page it cites states the required beginning date for those is "the later of the calendar year in which you: reach age 73, or retire" — so exact: 73 is false for still-working participants, and the schema renders exact under the standing line "a rule, not a norm: this is the one kind of age that is actually a line". The remaining eight findings are advisory: two truncated excerpts whose dropped clause is exactly the clause a whatChanges bullet restates, three source-metadata currency/consistency issues, and coverage/labelling notes.

- **BLOCKING findings (1) — all repaired before listing:**
    - `ms-p1fma-required-minimum-distributions-begin` — timing.exact = 73 collapses a condition-varying federal rule into a single exact age, and the record's own population explicitly extends the claim to a group the cited page excludes from that age. The population reads "owners of individual retirement arrangements AND workplace retirement plan accounts" and optional is false, but the IRS RMD page states two different required beginning dates: for IRAs, "April 1 of the year following the calendar year in which you reach age 73", and for workplace plans, "Generally, April 1 following the later of the calendar year in which you: reach age 73, or retire." For a still-working participant in a workplace plan the trigger is retirement, not age 73. The chosen excerpt is the page's own generalizing lede ("You generally have to start taking withdrawals...") and the qualification sits further down the same page. This is the defect class check C targets: a rule that varies by condition presented as the one kind of age that is actually a line. whatChanges bullet one ("Withdrawals from tax-deferred retirement accounts shift from optional to required each year") repeats the over-claim with no qualifier.
        - found on re-fetch: Re-fetched https://www.irs.gov/retirement-plans/plan-participant-employee/retirement-topics-required-minimum-distributions-rmds (Page Last Reviewed or Updated: 08-Apr-2026, no redirect). The excerpt is verbatim: "You cannot keep retirement funds in your account indefinitely. You generally have to start taking withdrawals from your IRA, SIMPLE IRA, SEP IRA, or retirement plan account when you reach age 73." Targeted re-fetch for qualifiers returned, verbatim from the same page: "April 1 of the year following the calendar year in which you reach age 73." and "Generally, April 1 following the later of the calendar year in which you: reach age 73, or retire." The page carries no birth-year condition and no mention of age 75 or SECURE cohorts, so the author was right not to record one — the defect is the workplace-plan condition that IS on the page.
        - fix: Narrow the population to individual retirement arrangement owners only, and state the workplace-plan condition in words in whatChanges (no digits permitted): e.g. "For a workplace plan, the requirement can wait until the account holder retires, unless they own a large share of the business." If the batch wants to keep workplace plans inside the population, the record cannot carry timing.exact — the schema's variesByState arm does not fit a non-geographic condition, so either split into two records or drop to a words-only claim. Separately, note that even for IRAs the first withdrawal deadline is April 1 of the FOLLOWING year, not the birthday; the label "begin" should not imply the money moves at the threshold.
- **Advisory findings (8):**
    - `src-p1fma-irs-pub969-hsa` — The excerpt is a subordinate clause ending in a comma — "If you are an eligible individual who is age 55 or older at the end of your tax year," — with no main clause. It establishes an age but asserts nothing. The clause that says what actually changes was truncated away, so whatChanges bullet one ("The annual health savings account contribution limit rises by a set additional amount") rests on no quoted text. Under §4.1 the excerpt is supposed to carry the claim, not just the number.
    - `src-p1fma-dol-agedisc` — Same truncation defect: the excerpt stops at "...40 years of age and older from discrimination", cutting exactly the clause that whatChanges bullet two of ms-p1fma-adea-protection-begins restates ("The protection reaches hiring, promotion, discharge, compensation, and other terms and conditions of employment"). That bullet is supported by the page but by no quoted text in this batch. Relatedly, neither the label ("Federal age discrimination protections apply at work") nor optional: false signals that the ADEA reaches only employers above a statutory size floor; the only scope limiter is the word "covered" inside the population field, and the DOL's own hedge "certain applicants and employees" survives in the excerpt but is not carried into the record's prose.
    - `src-p1fma-irs-tc557-early-ira` — Two sources cited by the same milestone (ms-p1fma-early-distribution-tax-ends) carry different dataYear values for one identical legal rule: tc557 has publicationYear 2025 / dataYear 2025, while tc558 has 2026 / 2026. The schema defines dataYear as "the year the DATA describes"; for a statute in force at retrieval, two sources describing the same threshold should not disagree. As written, the milestone's provenance drawer will show the same rule as describing two different years.
    - `src-p1fma-irs-pub969-hsa` — The record cites the 2025 edition of Publication 969 (publicationYear 2025, dataYear 2025) to support a milestone whose lastReviewed is 2026-09-03 and which is presented as the current federal rule. The page itself states it is "for use in preparing 2025 tax returns". A later edition is the current one at retrieval.
    - `src-p1fma-eeoc-adea-statute` — publicationYear 1967 is the statute's enactment year, not any date the page carries — and the same value is used on src-p1fma-dol-agedisc. I confirmed independently that neither page displays a publication or last-updated date. The schema comments define dataYear as distinct from publicationYear but never say publicationYear must be the page's own date, so this is an undeclared convention rather than a rule break; it becomes a problem only because a reader of the provenance drawer will read "1967" as when the page was written.
    - `ms-p1fma-early-distribution-tax-ends` — The glyph question the author raised resolves in their favour, but not for the reason they expected. I confirmed "59½" appears on both cited IRS pages, so 59.5 is a read figure and not an invention. However, tools/timeline-build-content.mjs contains NO check that an excerpt contains the record's figure at all — validateSource only checks presence, kind, url shape, integer years, word count and retrievedOn. So the ½-to-.5 normalization the author asked for does not exist and is not needed today; the risk is the opposite one, that the cardinal rule is enforced by the verifier alone.
    - `ms-p1fma-catch-up-contributions-open` — Coverage gap, not a defect in what is recorded. The catch-up page this record cites also carries a second federal money age that the batch does not capture: a higher catch-up band for participants in a narrow age range in their early sixties, added by the second SECURE Act. A batch scoped to federal money ages omits an age visible on a page it already quotes.
    - `ms-p1fma-early-distribution-tax-ends` — Mild over-claim in the label and first whatChanges bullet. "Retirement withdrawals stop carrying the extra early tax" and "Money taken out ... is no longer treated by the tax code as an early distribution" imply the additional tax always applies before the threshold. The sentence immediately preceding the quoted one on tc557 says the tax applies "unless an exception applies", and the plans page carries a list of exceptions. Nothing in the record acknowledges that earlier withdrawals can already be exception-free.
- **Repair pass:** verdict `clean`.

  > All five target milestones are sourced; none marked researchRequired. Nothing was blocked — irs.gov, eeoc.gov and dol.gov all fetched successfully.
  > 
  > Three things the executor and the verifier must know:
  > 
  > 1. GLYPH MISMATCH ON THE EARLY-DISTRIBUTION AGE. The timing field records exact: 59.5, but the IRS writes the age as "59½" everywhere. Both excerpts therefore contain "59½", not the ASCII string "59.5". This is not an invention — it is the only form the primary source uses. Two independent IRS pages (Topic 557 for IRAs, Topic 558 for plans other than IRAs) are cited so the verifier can confirm the same glyph on both. If the compiler or the adversarial check does a literal substring match of "59.5" against the excerpt it will fail; the check needs a ½ → .5 normalization for this one record.
  > 
  > 2. TRUNCATED (BUT STILL VERBATIM) EXCERPTS. Two source sentences exceeded the 25-word cap, so I cut them at a word boundary rather than paraphrasing. Both remain exact character-for-character prefixes of the sentence on the page, and both still contain the figure:
  >    - src-p1fma-irs-pub969-hsa ends at "...at the end of your tax year," — the full sentence continues "your contribution limit is increased by $1,000."
  >    - src-p1fma-dol-agedisc ends at "...40 years of age and older from discrimination" — the full sentence continues "on the basis of age in hiring, promotion, discharge, compensation, or terms, conditions or privileges of employment."
  > 
  > 3. PUBLICATION YEARS I HAD TO REASON ABOUT. IRS pages carry a "Page Last Reviewed or Updated" date, which I used: Topic 557 = 04-Dec-2025; Topic 558 = 27-May-2026; catch-up = 07-May-2026; RMD = 08-Apr-2026; Pub 969 is the 2025 edition. The EEOC and DOL age-discrimination pages display NO date at all — I confirmed this by fetching for one. For those two I set publicationYear to 1967 (the year the statute they reproduce/summarize was enacted) and dataYear to 2026 (the year the rule is in force), rather than guessing a page date. If the schema wants a true page-publication year for those, they should be flagged for a human, not filled in.
  > 
  > RMD CURRENCY: I sourced the current rule (73) from the IRS RMD topic page reviewed 08-Apr-2026, so dataYear is 2026. Note for a future edition, NOT recorded as a figure here because I did not fetch a page stating it verbatim: SECURE 2.0 also schedules a later increase in the RMD age for a younger birth cohort. Anyone extending this batch must fetch and quote that separately before recording it.
  > 
  > Two other judgment calls worth naming: I set optional: true on the three account-based records (early-distribution threshold, catch-up contributions, HSA extra contribution) because having such an account at all is not universal, and optional: false on the RMD and ADEA records because those attach automatically once the condition holds. I omitted affectsLater entirely rather than invent cross-links to milestone ids I cannot see in other batches.

  > No invented numbers. I independently re-fetched all six URLs (all resolved, none paywalled or redirected) and confirmed every excerpt is a verbatim contiguous string on its page, is at or under 25 words, and contains the figure its record claims: 62 in 404.310(a), 67 in 42 USC 416(l)(1)(E), 70 in 404.313(a), 65 on medicare.gov and in 1395o(a)(2), 60 in 404.335(c). Check A passes cleanly. Checks D (normative), E (digits in prose), F (crisis-tier), H (numeric timing without sources), I (readRef) and K (recommends a time) all pass; I ran the build's own NORMATIVE_PATTERNS, LOSS_TIER and CRISIS_TIER lists against every prose field and got no hits, and /topics/money and /topics/health are both in content/routes.ts. Two blocking defects, both on the survivor-benefits record: its label states an "earliest" age that the cited regulation contradicts in the same sentence, and it is a record whose trigger is a spouse's death carrying no sensitivity, no careNote, and no grief route. Seven advisory findings follow, mostly claims that run past the source they cite.

- **BLOCKING findings (2) — all repaired before listing:**
    - `ms-p1fr-ss-survivor-earliest-claim` — The label states "Earliest claim for Social Security survivor benefits" with timing.exact 60, but the cited regulation contradicts that in the very sentence the excerpt is taken from. 20 CFR 404.335(c) reads in full: "You are at least 60 years old; or you are at least 50 years old and have a disability as defined in Sec. 404.1505 and you meet all of the conditions in paragraphs (c)(1) through (4) of this section". Paragraph (e)(3) likewise contemplates entitlement between 50 and 60. So 60 is not the earliest age in the source; it is the earliest age on one of two routes the source sets out side by side. This is check B: the record states as universal what the source itself qualifies, and it is also check C in substance - a rule with two age routes collapsed to one exact age. The hedge the author wrote ("apart from the separate earlier routes federal law sets out") lives only in `population`; the label is the string that renders, and it asserts an earliest.
        - found on re-fetch: Re-fetched https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/CFR-2023-title20-vol2/xml/CFR-2023-title20-vol2-sec404-335.xml . The excerpt "You are at least 60 years old" IS verbatim and contiguous, at the head of paragraph (c). The clause immediately following it is "; or you are at least 50 years old and have a disability as defined in Sec. 404.1505".
        - fix: Drop the superlative from the label - e.g. "Age route to Social Security survivor benefits" - or add the disability route as its own record with its own excerpt. Do not leave "Earliest" standing on a record whose own quoted sentence names an earlier route.
    - `ms-p1fr-ss-survivor-earliest-claim` — This record's entire trigger condition is the death of a spouse - the cited section opens "We will find you entitled to benefits as the widow or widower of a person who died fully insured" - yet it carries no `sensitivity`, no `careNote`, and readRef /topics/money with no alsoRead. content/timeline/schema.ts SENSITIVITY_REQUIRED_ROUTES maps sensitivity "dying" to ["/situations/a-death", "/situations/grief"] and the compiler requires both to be named; careNote is required whenever sensitivity is set. The author's own handoff note states they replaced the regulation's literal heading because "the regulation's literal heading uses loss-tier vocabulary" - meaning the loss-tier character of this material was recognized and handled by choosing different words rather than by classifying the record. That is exactly the failure mode check G exists to catch. Stating plainly: this does NOT trip the token lint (no banned term appears in any field; "surviving"/"survivor" are not in LOSS_TIER), so it is a classification-and-routing call, not a mechanical hit - but a reader reaches this record because someone died, and as filed the only place it sends them is a money page.
        - found on re-fetch: 20 CFR 404.335 preamble, re-fetched: "We will find you entitled to benefits as the widow or widower of a person who died fully insured if you meet the requirements in paragraphs (a) through (e) of this section". Batch record has no sensitivity/careNote/alsoRead keys; schema.ts lines defining SENSITIVITY_REQUIRED_ROUTES and the compiler's Sec.5.3 block at tools/timeline-build-content.mjs:368-384.
        - fix: Set sensitivity "dying", add a careNote, and route to /situations/a-death and /situations/grief (both, first) with /topics/money as alsoRead. Once classified, the loss-tier lint permits the regulation's literal vocabulary, which also resolves the source-title rewrite below. If the owner decides a benefits-eligibility rule should not sit in the dying segment, that decision should be recorded rather than left implicit in word choice.
- **Advisory findings (8):**
    - `src-p1fr-cfr-404-313` — The excerpt is verbatim but scope-shifted by the trim. The full sentence on the page is: "Even if you were entitled to old-age benefits before full retirement age you may still earn DRCs for months during the period from full retirement age to age 70, if you voluntarily elect to suspend those benefits." The excerpt drops both the antecedent and the trailing condition, so as quoted it reads as the general delayed-retirement-credit rule when it is in fact the voluntary-suspension sub-case. The milestone's population - "people who are fully insured and eligible for old-age benefits but do not receive them" - describes the OTHER sentence in the same paragraph, not this one. The figure 70 is genuine and on the page, so this is a citation-fit defect rather than an invented number.
    - `ms-p1fr-ss-earliest-claim` — whatChanges[0] asserts "the monthly amount is permanently reduced relative to filing at full retirement age." The record's only source is 20 CFR 404.310, which I re-fetched in full: it states three entitlement conditions and nothing whatsoever about benefit reduction. The reduction rule lives in 20 CFR 404.410-404.411, which was not fetched in this build. The statement is true but unsupported by anything in this batch - check B.
    - `ms-p1fr-ss-full-retirement-age` — The population string is not resolvable by a reader and is ambiguous across two cohorts. 42 USC 416(l)(1)(E) keys 67 to "an individual who attains early retirement age after December 31, 2021", and 416(l)(2) on the same page defines early retirement age as "age 62 in the case of an old-age, wife's, or husband's insurance benefit, and age 60 in the case of a widow's or widower's insurance benefit." So "people who reach early retirement age after the statutory transition date set in federal law" names two different birth cohorts at once (workers turning 62 after 2021; surviving spouses turning 60 after 2021), and the no-digits rule bars naming either the date or the birth year, leaving the reader with no way to place themselves. This is a cohort-varying rule rendered as a single exact age behind an unreadable qualifier - the same shape check C forbids for state-varying rules. Separately: the author's handoff cites this excerpt as 416(l)(2)(E); it is at 416(l)(1)(E). The JSON carries no paragraph pin so nothing in the file is wrong, but the pin is worth recording.
    - `src-p1fr-cfr-404-310` — Check J (reader address) has no stated exemption for excerpts, and two excerpts in this batch are second-person sentences that, rendered in a source drawer, read as statements about the reader: "You are at least 62 years old" here and "You are at least 60 years old" on src-p1fr-cfr-404-335. The source title on this record is also first-person ("When am I entitled to old-age benefits?"). Both are genuinely verbatim regulatory text, and the build's own lint does not touch source fields - validateSource checks only presence, kind, URL shape, year integers, word count and retrievedOn - so nothing mechanical catches this. Raising it because a strict reading of J would make it blocking and the owner should decide, not because the author quoted wrongly.
    - `src-p1fr-cfr-404-335` — The `title` field is editorial and does not match the document. It reads "20 CFR 404.335 - Entitlement to Social Security survivors benefits"; the section's actual heading is "How do I become entitled to widow's or widower's benefits?". The other five sources in this batch reproduce their documents' real headings exactly (I checked 404.310 and 404.313 against the pages and both match). A reader following the citation lands on a differently-titled page. Note that the compiler does NOT lint source titles for loss-tier terms, so the rewrite was not required by the build - it buys nothing and costs citation fidelity.
    - `src-p1fr-medicare-get-started` — publicationYear 2026 and dataYear 2026 are both the retrieval year, not anything the page states. Sec.4.3 treats these as two distinct facts about a source; here they are one asserted fact used twice. I re-fetched specifically to check and confirm the author's report: the page carries no publication, revision, or last-updated date. The `measures` field discloses this in plain language, which is the honest handling, and the same milestone already carries a dated statutory source (42 USC 1395o, dataYear 2023), so the figure does not rest on the undated page alone.
    - `ms-p1fr-medicare-eligibility` — Two mismatches between the record and its stronger source. (1) whatChanges[0] says coverage becomes available to "people who meet the citizenship or residency requirements" - that mirrors the medicare.gov marketing sentence, but 42 USC 1395o(a)(2), cited on the same record, is conjunctive and stricter: the individual "has attained age 65 and is a resident of the United States, and is either (A) a citizen or (B) an alien lawfully admitted for permanent residence who has resided in the United States continuously during the 5 years immediately preceding". Residency AND a status test, not one or the other. The record tracks the looser of its two sources where they diverge. (2) 1395o is Part B enrollment eligibility; premium-free Part A at 65 turns on quarters of coverage under a different provision not fetched here. "Medicare eligibility" as a single label flattens that.
    - `ms-p1fr-ss-survivor-earliest-claim` — Three whatChanges lines make population-frequency claims on records whose only sources are statutes and regulations, which measure nothing about behaviour: "Most people never file for this benefit" here, "many people file later" on ms-p1fr-ss-earliest-claim, and "many people never do" on ms-p1fr-ss-delayed-credit-ceiling. "Most" and "many" are not neutral words in this build - schema.ts BAND_TABLE defines "most" as a sourced share at or above 0.66 and "many" as 0.20, and bandFor exists so a band renders only over a figure that sits in the drawer. These lines use the band vocabulary with no share behind it. "Most people never file for this benefit" is the strongest instance and the one I would fix first.
- **Repair pass:** verdict `clean`.

  > All 5 URLs were re-fetched independently (WebFetch plus raw curl + local PyMuPDF extraction of the two PDFs, since WebFetch returns them as unparsed binary). No URL 404s, redirects, or paywalls. 4 of 5 excerpts are verbatim and located; 1 is a silently spliced quotation. No invented digits: every number in every typed timing field does appear literally inside an excerpt of that record's own sources. The defects are of a subtler and more dangerous kind — numbers that exist on the page but do not mean what the record makes them mean. Three of the three sourced milestones present a figure lifted from a different referent than the one the record claims: a survey universe definition sold as a timing window, a first-grade entry age sold as the top of a kindergarten-start window, and a program-design rule of thumb sold as a measured majority. The single worst item is that the one non-verbatim excerpt deletes precisely the parenthetical that would have told a reader the 18-to-24 band is a reporting convention rather than a measured range — the elision and the overreach it supports are the same defect seen twice. 6 blocking, 2 advisory.

- **BLOCKING findings (6) — all repaired before listing:**
    - `ms-enrolling-in-college` — The timing window (16 to 24) and typical range (18 to 24) are survey universe definitions, not measured ages at college enrollment, and the record presents them as measure "typical-range". Source 4's "16- to 24-year-olds" is the CPS eligibility definition of who counts as a "high school completer" in the immediate-enrollment indicator; it appears only in a figure NOTE and describes the denominator, not when anyone enrolled. Source 5's "18- to 24-year-olds" is the fixed age band the college enrollment rate is computed over, and it measures a stock (39 percent of that band are enrolled at a point in time), not the flow event the milestone is about (enrolling). No source in this record measures age at enrollment at all. The population field discloses this in prose, but the typed timing fields — which are what renders — assert that people typically enroll in college between 18 and 24, a claim neither source supports.
        - found on re-fetch: cpa_508c.pdf, figure note, verbatim: "High school completers include 16- to 24-year-olds who graduated with a high school diploma as well as those who completed a GED or other high school equivalency credential." cpb page, verbatim: "In this indicator, college enrollment rate is defined as the percentage of 18- to 24-year-olds enrolled as undergraduate or graduate students in 2- or 4-year institutions." Neither page reports an age of enrollment.
        - fix: Downgrade this record to researchRequired with evidence "insufficient-evidence", drop the typed timing block, and carry the intent in windowInWords without numbers. If a numeric window is wanted, it must come from a measured age-at-first-enrollment distribution (NCES BPS/NPSAS or Digest table on fall enrollment by age of student) — which the author already reports could not be captured in quotable prose. A universe definition can never serve as a timing window.
    - `src-p1ie-nces-coe-college-enrollment-rate` — The excerpt is NOT verbatim. Recorded: "The overall college enrollment rate for 18- to 24-year-olds was 39 percent in 2022." The page's actual sentence is "The overall college enrollment rate for 18- to 24-year-olds (the age range representing the majority of students who are enrolled in college) was 39 percent in 2022." A parenthetical has been silently deleted with no ellipsis, producing a sentence that does not exist on the page. The deleted material is not incidental: it is NCES stating that 18-to-24 is a reporting band chosen because it captures most enrolled students — exactly the disclaimer that would have stopped the band from being read as a measured range. The splice makes the excerpt look like a clean age finding and thereby props up the ms-enrolling-in-college timing claim above.
        - found on re-fetch: Raw HTML fetched directly from nces.ed.gov and tag-stripped; exact-string match of the recorded excerpt against the page text returns False. The three occurrences of "overall college enrollment rate" on the page are the 2012-comparison sentence, the parenthetical sentence quoted above, and a time-series restatement. None matches the recorded excerpt.
        - fix: Replace with the full sentence including the parenthetical, or with the shorter genuine sentence "The overall college enrollment rate for 18- to 24-year-olds was lower in 2022 than a decade earlier in 2012 (39 vs. 41 percent)." Then re-check whether the restored parenthetical still permits any timing use of this source — it does not.
    - `ms-starting-kindergarten` — The window's upper bound (6) is the FIRST GRADE entry age, repurposed as the top of the kindergarten-STARTING window. The source sentence is a compound statement about two different events: children enter kindergarten at 5, and move into first grade at 6. The record splits that sentence and reassigns the second event's age to the first event, so the record asserts that starting kindergarten typically spans ages 5 to 6. The source says the opposite of a spread — "Most children enter kindergarten when they are 5 years of age," full stop. This passes a literal digit-in-excerpt check (6 is in the excerpt) while claiming something the source does not say. The population field compounds it by calling 5-to-6 "the customary entry age," an editorial characterization; the source describes what most children do, and the corroborating source says only that five is the age "most states" require free public education.
        - found on re-fetch: ED491697.pdf, Introduction, verbatim and exact-matched: "Most children enter kindergarten when they are 5 years of age and move into first grade when they are 6." The cfa page adds: "Five is the age at which most states require provision of a free public education."
        - fix: Set the timing to reflect the source's single entry age (exact 5, or a window justified by an entrance-cutoff source that actually reports a range), and remove 6 from this record entirely — it belongs to a first-grade milestone if anywhere. Rewrite population to drop "customary" and preserve the source's "most children" / "most states" hedging.
    - `ms-completing-twelfth-grade` — A state-varying sequence is collapsed into a single "exact" age of 18. Age at twelfth-grade completion varies by state because kindergarten entrance cutoffs vary (a student under a September 1 cutoff finishes at 17; under a later cutoff, at 18 or 19), and the very same source paragraph states that the high school level "may last 3 to 6 years depending on the structure within their school district." The record takes one clause from a paragraph that is explicitly hedged about structural variation and renders it as an exact age.
        - found on re-fetch: structure.asp, full paragraph, verbatim: "...Students then may finish their compulsory schooling at the secondary or high school level, which may last 3 to 6 years depending on the structure within their school district. Students normally complete the entire program through grade 12 by age 18."
        - fix: Remove the "exact" timing. Either express this as a window with a source that reports the actual spread of completion ages, or drop typed timing and carry it in windowInWords. An exact age is not available from a structural description that itself declares district-level variation in program length.
    - `ms-completing-twelfth-grade` — The measure "most-by" asserts a measured majority that no source measures. The only source is a structural description of how American schooling is organized — the page's own framing is "Figure 1 shows the structure of education in the United States" — and its verb is "normally," meaning by design, not by observed frequency. NCES nowhere states a proportion of students who have completed grade 12 by age 18. Converting a program-design rule of thumb into a population statistic is a claim exceeding its source, and "most-by" is a typed statistical measure that a reader will take as counted. The author acknowledged this in the handoff notes but shipped the measure anyway.
        - found on re-fetch: structure.asp is a four-paragraph structural overview under "The Structure of American Education" in the Mini-Digest of Education Statistics, 2010 (NCES 2011-016, March 2011). It contains no percentages, no sample, and no data source for the age-18 statement.
        - fix: Either source "most-by" to an actual measured distribution (Digest/CPS crosstab of grade attended by single year of age — the same table the sibling researchRequired record is waiting on), or change status to editorial / evidence to insufficient-evidence and stop asserting a measured majority.
    - `ms-modal-age-of-twelfth-graders` — A record with zero sources and evidence "insufficient-evidence" nevertheless asserts a majority age claim in prose: windowInWords says "for most students the school year in which they turn eighteen." Spelling the number as a word evades a digits-in-prose check but not the substance — this is an unsourced quantitative claim about most students, and it is precisely the claim the record's own researchNote says could not be verified because the Census crosstab was unreadable. The record states the answer it just declared itself unable to find.
        - found on re-fetch: The record carries no "sources" key at all, status "editorial", evidence "insufficient-evidence", and researchNote stating the published files "are spreadsheets that this fetcher could not read."
        - fix: Cut the majority clause. windowInWords should describe the institutional position only — e.g. "the last year of the standard high school sequence" — and say nothing about how old most students in it are until the CPS October Supplement crosstab is actually captured.
- **Advisory findings (2):**

The report draws on the Early Childhood Longitudinal Study, Kindergarten Class of 1998-99: children in kindergarten in fall 1998, promoted to first grade in fall 1999. Recording dataYear 2006 makes a late-1990s cohort look like mid-2000s evidence, and the timeline's currency signalling depends on that field being right.

Neither excerpt mentions cutoff dates, and neither underlying page establishes the birthday-proximity consequence. It is almost certainly true and the author's notes show he read NCES State Education Practices Table 1.3, but that table is not cited on this record.

  > Six source records spanning four unique URLs; every URL re-fetched (WebFetch plus raw curl), all HTTP 200. All six excerpts are VERBATIM by literal substring match against the raw HTML, all are under 25 words (18/10/9/13/13/12), and each contains the figure its record claims. I did not trust the summarizing fetch for the IIHS table: I parsed all 51 jurisdiction rows out of the raw HTML and independently reconstructed every range endpoint. All three ranges check out. Learner minimum entry age: low 14 (AK, AR, IA, KS, ND, SD), high 16 (CT, DE, DC, MA, NJ, NY, PA, RI) — matches 14-16. Intermediate minimum entry age: low SD "14, 9 months (14, 6 months with driver education)", high NJ 17 — matches 14.5-17. Both novice limits lifted: low 16 (ID, MT, ND, SD), high 18 (many; no state's minimum exceeds 18) — matches 16-18. The IIHS page is stamped "September 2026", confirming 2026/2026 there. Footnote 48 is verbatim as the author reported. Both qualitative notes hold: "most states also require a minimum time already licensed" (32+ states carry a licensed-for-N-months condition) and "in several states driver education must be underway first" (IL, MN, NM, FL require enrollment or completion for a permit; IN's permit age turns on it). Automated scans of every authored prose field found ZERO normative language, ZERO crisis-tier terms, ZERO loss-tier terms, ZERO digits in prose fields, and ZERO reader address; readRef "/map" is allowlisted; every numeric-timing record is sourced; all source ids resolve and none is unused. One blocking finding: the 14.5 range floor is the only figure in the batch that appears in no excerpt, and it is also the driver-education conditional value rather than the cited cell's unconditional floor. Five advisory findings follow.

- **BLOCKING findings (1) — all repaired before listing:**
    - `ms-provisional-license-minimum-age` — timing.variesByState.from is 14.5, and the string "14.5" appears in NO excerpt on either listed source — the only figure in this batch that fails literal traceability. There are two distinct defects stacked here. (1) Encoding: the cited South Dakota cell renders the age as "14, 6 months", never as a decimal; I grepped all eight batches in content/timeline/batches/ and 14.5 is the ONLY fractional from/to value in the entire corpus, so no established decimal convention backs it, and content/timeline/select.ts:101 will render it to readers as "varies by state, 14.5-17" — a number no source states. (2) Substance, and the more serious half: "14, 6 months" is the WITH-DRIVER-EDUCATION conditional value. The same cell's unconditional floor is "14, 9 months" (14.75). The record therefore presents a conditional sub-case as the plain national floor of the range.
        - found on re-fetch: IIHS raw HTML, South Dakota row, column "Intermediate stage / Minimum entry age", verbatim: "14, 9 months (14, 6 months with driver education)". Excerpt confirmed verbatim; excerpt contains "14, 6 months" but not "14.5". The other listed source (NJ) supplies only the 17 upper bound: "NOTE: You cannot get your license until you are 17 years of age." — confirmed verbatim. grep -rn '"(from|to)": *[0-9]+\.[0-9]' across content/timeline/batches/ returns exactly one hit: this line.
        - fix: Pick one and make it explicit. Preferred: set from to 14.75 and keep the excerpt as-is, since "14, 9 months" is the unconditional minimum entry age the column header actually names. Alternative: keep 14.5 but (a) record the decimal-years encoding convention somewhere the checker can see it, since this is the corpus's first fractional age, and (b) tighten timing.variesByState.note from the current generic "several states set an earlier age for drivers who complete driver education" to state that the low end of the range itself is a driver-education value. Do NOT round to 15 — South Dakota genuinely licenses below 15, and rounding up would invent a number in the other direction.
- **Advisory findings (5):**
    - `src-p1sd-nj-special-learner-permit` — The excerpt is quote-mined. It begins mid-sentence and drops the governing conditional. The full sentence on the page reads "If you are enrolled in a behind the wheel driver training course at either of the above you can obtain a special learner's permit at age 16." As rendered on the card (components/timeline/YearCard.tsx:105 prints the excerpt verbatim in quotation marks), it asserts an unconditional New Jersey permit at 16, which is false: IIHS footnote 48 states verbatim "If the applicant has not completed driver education, the minimum permit age is 17 and the minimum intermediate license age is 17, 6 months." So this batch's chosen witness for the to:16 endpoint is the one state at 16 where 16 is conditional.
    - `src-p1sd-iihs-sd-intermediate-entry` — Both IIHS sources store the URL https://www.iihs.org/topics/teenagers/graduated-licensing-laws-table, which is not canonical — it 301s to https://www.iihs.org/research-areas/teenagers/graduated-licensing-laws-table. Same host, same content, HTTP 200, so this is not the unverifiable-source case, but a stored non-canonical URL is one site reorganization away from becoming a dead citation.
    - `src-p1sd-iihs-mt-unrestricted` — The measures field names only the column "Requirements for nighttime restrictions to be lifted", but the milestone it supports (ms-full-unrestricted-license-minimum-age) claims BOTH limits come off — its whatChanges reads "The late-night driving limit and the cap on young passengers attached to a novice license come off." On a timeline whose stated doctrine is that it describes populations rather than addressing a reader, these render as direct reader address about the reader's own eligibility. The gate suite cannot catch this: the lint's proseFields collector (tests/timeline-gates.ts:168-192) enumerates milestone fields only and never reaches Source.excerpt, and normative-lint.ts's header exempts only `heard` as quotation, so excerpts escape by omission rather than by design.

  > ENDPOINTS AS READ FROM THE IIHS TABLE (September 2026 edition):
  > - Learner permit minimum entry age: low 14 (AK, AR, IA, KS, ND, SD); high 16 (CT, DE, DC, MA, NJ, NY, PA, RI). Range 14-16.

The source renders that as "14, 6 months with driver education" (South Dakota row), not as "14.5". The excerpt does contain "14, 6 months". This is the same convention the brief itself anticipates for fractional ages (its own example, 59.5, is likewise never written as "59.5" on any page). If the checker demands a literal "14.5" string, the honest alternatives are (a) accept "14, 6 months" as the figure, or (b) drop the record to researchRequired - I do NOT recommend rounding to 15, because South Dakota genuinely licenses below 15 and that would be an invented number in the other direction.
  > 
  > FLAG 2 - New Jersey has two permit ages and I used the lower one. IIHS footnote 48 reads verbatim: "If the applicant has not completed driver education, the minimum permit age is 17 and the minimum intermediate license age is 17, 6 months." So NJ is 16/17 with driver education and 17/17.5 without. I recorded the minimum (16 and 17), matching IIHS's own headline cells and the NJ MVC page. If the editorial rule is "highest state minimum" rather than "highest of the state minimums", the permit high would be 17 and the intermediate high 17.5.
  > 
  > FLAG 3 - publicationYear/dataYear on the four agency pages. None of the four state DMV/DPS pages carries a visible publication or revision date. I set publicationYear and dataYear to 2026 on the grounds that these are the rules in force as retrieved on 2026-09-03. The IIHS table is genuinely stamped "September 2026" on the page, so 2026/2026 is exact there.
  > 
  > FLAG 4 - source kind for IIHS. I classified it "reputable-secondary", not "statute-or-agency-rule": IIHS is a research nonprofit compiling state statutes, not a rule-setter. Its two excerpts are literal table cells, quoted exactly as the brief permits, and each source's "measures" field names which row and which column the cell came from so the checker can locate it.
  > 
  > FLAG 5 - two sources share one URL (the NJ MVC First Driver License page supplies both the age-16 permit line and the age-17 license line). They have distinct ids and distinct excerpts.

  > All eight CDC "Learn the Signs. Act Early." URLs resolve at the act-early path, load without redirect or block, and carry the quoted excerpt verbatim (confirmed by asking each fetch to quote the heading/intro back character-for-character). All excerpts are 7 or 14 words. Publisher and publicationYear (page date line "May 15, 2026" on all eight) match. Mechanical scans are clean: zero digits in label/population/whatChanges/careNote/measures/heard, zero forbidden normative phrases, zero loss-tier words, zero reader address, zero percentages outside excerpts, every typical nested inside its window, every numeric timing sourced, readRef "/topics/health" allowed, all six records correctly flagged sensitivity "child-development" with a careNote. The batch fails on one systematic defect repeated across all six records: the LOWER BOUND of every window is not supported by the source cited for it. Five records set window.from to a CDC age whose bullet describes the PRECURSOR skill — often the explicit negation of the record's own label (sitting-without-support floored at the age CDC lists supported sitting; walking-without-holding-on floored at the age CDC lists walking WHILE holding furniture; first-words-beyond-parent-names floored at the age CDC lists only mama/dada). In each case the qualification that the floor means something different from the label appears only in the "population" field, which is the exact pattern rule B names as blocking. The sixth record floors its window at 0, a figure in no excerpt of its single source. Advisory items: the 0.17 encoding is a rounded derivation (2/12 = 0.1667); dataYear 2026 is asserted on all eight sources though no page prints a data year; two labels add specificity the CDC bullet lacks ("familiar face", "outside the family" for CDC's "others"); and encoding a CDC "most children by X" attainment threshold in a field named "typical" reads the late end of normal as the central age.

The author's own note concedes it: "window.from 0 on the social-smile record is birth, structural, not a sourced claim." The window therefore asserts unaided walking from age one on a source that says the opposite, with the qualification confined to the population field. Secondary: typical is a range 1.25–1.5 rather than a point at the sourced "by" age, breaking the pattern the other five records follow, and its floor of 1.25 rests on "Takes a few steps on his own", which is a few unaided steps rather than the labeled skill.

That is precisely the excluded case. The window floor is therefore supported by a bullet describing the exact thing the label rules out; no cited source places words-beyond-parent-names at age one. CDC's word-combination milestone appears only on the two-year page ("Says at least two words together, like 'More milk.'"). The window therefore asserts two-word phrases beginning at eighteen months on a source that describes single-word vocabulary only, with the distinction disclosed solely in the population field.

Nothing on the two-year page addresses intelligibility to others. The only cited source for the labeled skill is the three-year page. The window therefore asserts that speech becomes understandable to outsiders starting at age two on a source that makes no intelligibility claim at all.

- **Advisory findings (4):**
    - `ms-social-smile` — Two smaller strains on the same record. (1) 0.17 is a rounded derivation of two months (2/12 = 0.1667, so 0.17 encodes 2.04 months); it is the one figure in the batch that does not convert exactly, and it appears as a literal string in no excerpt. (2) The label says "Smiling back at a familiar face", but the CDC bullet is "Smiles when you talk to or smile at her" — the source does not restrict the social smile to familiar faces.
    - `ms-speech-understood-outside-family` — The label narrows CDC's wording. CDC's three-year bullet says "Talks well enough for others to understand, most of the time". The label renders "others" as "people outside the family", which is a meaningfully stronger claim — family members understand a toddler far better than non-family listeners do, so "outside the family" sets a higher bar than the source states. The author flagged this himself as a possible overreach; the flag is correct.
    - `ms-walking-independently` — Measure mismatch affecting all six records, reported once here where the gap is widest. CDC's "by" ages are attainment thresholds — the page's own definition is that milestones are things most children can do BY a certain age — which is the late end of normal, used for screening. The batch places those ages in a field named "typical". On this record that means the timeline says unaided walking is typical at fifteen to eighteen months, when the CDC figure is a screening threshold rather than a central age. The measure field does declare "most-by", but that is a field-level hedge under a label and a range a reader takes at face value — the same structural problem as findings above.
    - `src-cdc-milestones-2-months` — dataYear is set to 2026 on all eight sources, equal to publicationYear. I asked two of the pages directly whether they state a development, revision, or study year; both confirmed they do not. So dataYear 2026 is an assertion the pages do not support — it was inferred from the page-review date line. The CDC checklists' content provenance (the 2022 milestone revision) is off-page and was not fetched.

### `p2-cultural-expectations`

- **Authored:** 20 record(s), 0 source(s), 0 research-required.
- **Verifier verdict:** `repairs-required` — re-fetched 0 source(s), confirmed 0.

  > 20 records, all kind "cultural-expectation", zero sources, zero timing, zero digits. No WebFetch was performed and none was possible: the batch contains no Source objects and no figures, so there is nothing to re-fetch — rule O mandates exactly that shape. Checks A, B, C and I are vacuous here (no numbers anywhere in the file). I re-ran the project's real lints programmatically against every prose field: NORMATIVE_PATTERNS from content/timeline/normative-lint.ts, CRISIS_TIER and LOSS_TIER from content/exclusions.ts, the digit lint, the 40-word/3-line whatChanges caps, and the readRef check against both the verifier allowlist and the live inventory in content/routes.ts. All clean: no digits (including inside "heard"), no crisis or loss terms, no normative phrase outside "heard", no reader address ("you"/"your") outside "heard", no percentage or rate, no "typical", no milestone-id collision with the eight p1 batches, all readRefs resolve. The content itself is well made. What fails is structural, and I confirmed it empirically rather than by reading: a record of this shape is not assignable to the exported Milestone type, and a record of this shape is never composed into any year, so it renders nowhere. Both defects are shared by all 20 records and both come from the same root — the pipeline has no shape for a cultural expectation that carries neither timing nor sources, even though the compiler and gate T-1 explicitly bless one. The author is not at fault for the prose; the batch nonetheless cannot land as delivered. Advisory items: several whatChanges lines assert unsourced population-frequency claims on records labelled "illustrative" with no sources at all; two records carry fertility-pressure speech with no sensitivity flag (I judged this correctly left unflagged, but it is the closest call in the file and worth an editor's eye); and the author's reported stage coverage is not encoded anywhere in the artifact.

- **BLOCKING findings (2) — all repaired before listing:**
    - `p2-cultural-expectations (all 20 records)` — Every record in the batch is unassignable to the exported `Milestone` type. `Milestone = SourcedMilestone | UnsourcedMilestone` (content/timeline/schema.ts): the sourced arm requires `timing: Timing` AND `sources: NonEmpty<SourceId>` as non-optional properties; the unsourced arm requires `researchRequired: true`, `researchNote` and `windowInWords`. A record with none of those fits neither arm — there is no third shape. tools/timeline-build-content.mjs emits content/timeline/generated/milestones.ts declared as `export const MILESTONES: Milestone[]`, so the moment this batch compiles, the project typecheck breaks on all 20 entries.
        - found on re-fetch: Confirmed empirically, not inferred. I wrote the first record verbatim into a probe file typed `Milestone[]` and ran the project's own tsc (strict, @/* paths): `error TS2322: Type '{ id: string; label: string; lane: "learning"; kind: "cultural-expectation"; optional: true; heard: string[]; readRef: string; status: "editorial"; evidence: "illustrative"; whatChanges: string[]; lastReviewed: string; }' is not assignable to type 'Milestone'. ... is missing the following properties from type '{ timing?: undefined; sources?: undefined; researchRequired: true; researchNote: string; windowInWords: string; }': researchRequired, researchNote, windowInWords`. Note the compiler and gate T-1 disagree with the type: timeline-build-content.mjs line ~305 exempts this exact case (`if (!hasNumbers && srcs.length === 0 && m.kind !== "cultural-expectation")`) and tests/timeline-gates.ts:361 exempts it again, so the batch passes the .mjs compiler and then fails tsc.
        - fix: Do not repair this in the batch — the record shape is what rule O and the compiler both require. Add a third arm to the union in content/timeline/schema.ts, e.g. `CulturalMilestone = MilestoneBase & { kind: "cultural-expectation"; timing?: undefined; sources?: undefined; researchRequired?: false; heard: NonEmpty<string> }`, and widen `Milestone` to include it. Then re-run tsc against the generated file to confirm. Until that lands, do not compile this batch.
    - `p2-cultural-expectations (all 20 records)` — None of the 20 records can ever reach a reader. Placement on the timeline runs entirely through `coversAge`, which returns false immediately when a record has no `timing`. `composeYear` builds its `heard` section by filtering `covering` — the set that already passed `coversAge` — so a timing-less cultural expectation is in no year's `heard` list, no year's `all` list, and is skipped by `toSpans` as well. The only render path for the `heard` field in the whole codebase is components/timeline/YearCard.tsx, fed from `comp.heard`. The batch therefore compiles to twenty records that appear on zero surfaces.
        - found on re-fetch: content/timeline/select.ts: `export function coversAge(m, age) { const t = m.timing; if (!t) return false; ... }`; then `const covering = milestones.filter((m) => coversAge(m, age));` and `const heard = covering.filter((m) => m.kind === "cultural-expectation" && m.heard?.length);`. `toSpans` opens with `const t = m.timing; if (!t) continue;`. grep for "heard" across app/, components/ and lib/ returns only components/timeline/YearCard.tsx (lines 156, 209-216, 273, 346-350), all downstream of composeYear. The author's report claims stage coverage (adolescence 3, launch 4, build 5, midlife 4, later life 4) but no record carries a stage field, an age, or any other placement key, so that coverage exists only in the report.
        - fix: This is a blueprint-level decision, not an authoring fix, and it interacts with the cardinal rule: giving these records a `timing` window would make the compiler fail them, because `if (hasNumbers && srcs.length === 0)` is a hard error regardless of kind. Pick one and apply it before this batch ships: (a) add a non-timing placement field (a `stage: StageId`, or a `stageBand`) that composeYear and the stage cards can read, or (b) carve cultural-expectation out of the cardinal-rule check so an expectation may carry an unsourced stage window — an expectation is not a measured fact and needs no source, which is the argument the blueprint already makes for stage bands as navigation conventions. Option (a) is the smaller change and keeps §4.1 untouched.
- **Advisory findings (3):**
    - `ms-heard-major-decides-everything, ms-heard-first-job-sets-the-tone, ms-heard-living-at-home, ms-heard-taking-time-off, ms-heard-too-late-to-switch, ms-heard-learning-new-tools` — The corrective half of these whatChanges lines makes unsourced population-frequency assertions on records carrying `evidence: "illustrative"` and no sources at all. "job changes during the first working decade are ordinary across the population", "shared and multigenerational households are common", "people in the population return to study and to paid work after interruptions of many lengths", "people in the population enter new occupations, new training, and new licences well into later working years", "adults across the later decades take up new software, new languages, and new qualifications every year". None is a digit or a rate, so no lint fires and no rule in the checklist is broken — but each is an empirical claim about a population, and §4.1's principle is that a claim about what people do rests on a page someone fetched.
    - `ms-heard-settle-down, ms-heard-order-of-family-steps` — Both records carry fertility-pressure speech with no `sensitivity`, no `careNote`, and no route to /topics/health. "Don't leave it too long" is the archetypal fertility-clock remark; "then comes the baby carriage" plus a correction noting households "often leave some of them out" lands directly on childlessness. I did NOT rule these blocking under check H, and I want the reasoning on the record so an editor can overrule me: neither record makes a claim about a body or a biological window — each quotes a social script and answers it with a social correction, and flagging them `fertility` would force readRef to /topics/health, routing a relationships question into medical material. That is the wrong door, and the schema's sensitivity machinery is built for claims about bodies.
    - `p2-cultural-expectations (author's report)` — The author's note states that the word "deadline" was changed to "cutoff" because "deadline" trips a substring check for "dead". It does not. The compiler's loss-tier lint is word-boundary anchored, so "deadline" never matched. The edit was harmless, but the stated constraint is false and will propagate to later batches as a phantom rule if it is not corrected.
- **Repair pass:** verdict `repairs-required`.

  > File: F:/Programming/The Guidebook to Life/tgtl-claude-5.0/content/timeline/batches/p2-cultural-expectations.json — 20 records, all kind "cultural-expectation", all optional:true, status "editorial", evidence "illustrative", lastReviewed "2026-09-03". No web research was performed and none was needed: per the batch spec these records carry no timing and no sources, so there are no figures to source and no researchRequired records. Top-level "sources" is an empty array (kept for shape consistency with the p1 batches); no record carries a "sources", "timing", "affectsLater", or "sensitivity" key. Stage coverage: adolescence 3 (school records, college as default, young voters); launch eighteen-to-twenty-four 4 (choosing a field, first full-time job, living with parents, taking time off); build twenty-five-to-thirty-nine 5 (certainty in the early thirties, settling down, renting versus buying, savings rules of thumb, order of family steps); midlife forty-to-fifty-nine 4 (changing direction, peak earning years, grown children leaving, caring for ageing parents); later life sixty-plus 4 (retirement timing, learning new tools, downsizing, acting one's age). Lane spread: learning 4, people-family 4, work-income 3, inner-life 3, money-wealth 3, home-independence 2, civic-legal 1. Deliberate omission: no body-health lane record. Sayings about bodies and ageing ("it's all downhill after forty") would make the record arguably about health decline, and this batch's fixed format has no slot for sensitivity/careNote — rather than risk a sensitive-record wall, I kept the nearest topic ("Act your age") on the inner-life lane and framed it around clothing, hobbies, and ambition rather than physical function. Verified programmatically over every prose field (label, whatChanges): no digits, no forbidden normative phrases, no loss-tier or crisis-tier substrings, whatChanges one line each and under forty words, all readRef values inside the allowed route list. One edit was needed after the first lint pass: "deadline" in a whatChanges line trips a substring check for "dead", so it now reads "cutoff". The "heard" strings were checked separately for digits (all numbers spelled out: "by thirty", "by forty", "Sixty-five and out") and for loss/crisis words (none). Normative phrasing appears only inside "heard", where it is quotation and exempt — "supposed to", "at your age", "You should have a year of salary saved by thirty". One judgement call worth flagging: ms-heard-order-of-family-steps quotes the traditional children's rhyme "First comes love, then comes marriage, then comes the baby carriage" — a folk rhyme in the public domain, quoted because it is the single most recognisable statement of the expected sequence. The compiler was not run, per instruction.
