  > All 9 sources re-fetched and confirmed: every excerpt appears verbatim on the live page, all are <=25 words, all contain the figure they support, and publisher/publicationYear/dataYear/measures match the documents (one title-string mismatch, advisory). The batch also passes every mechanical compiler check (digits, normative lint, tier walls, routes, careNote/readRef, typical-inside-window, excerpt word counts, bySex.measures) - I re-implemented tools/timeline-build-content.mjs in Python because node 16 here cannot strip types, and it reports zero errors. The failures are all in the timing encoding, and they are systematic: in all five sourced records, timing.window is authored as the gap between two point estimates (two sexes, two county groups, a mean and a median, two age-group labels) instead of "the outer extent, including the tails" that schema.ts defines it to be. Because content/timeline/select.ts renders window as "(seen from-to)" and places a record on the spine only in the years inside window, each of these publishes an outer extent that its own cited document contradicts - most severely on ms-caring-for-an-adult-relative (49.4-51, when the same report's Figure 3 shows 46% of caregivers are 18-49 and 12% are 65-74) and ms-first-birth-mean-age (24.8-28.5, when the same NVSR reports 8.7% of first births to mothers under 20 and 12.5% to mothers 35+). Two records additionally violate AUTHORING.md §5.1 traps 1-2 (a survey age universe and a stock used as event timing) and §5's explicit median-encoding rule. Six blocking findings, three advisory.

- **BLOCKING findings (6) — all repaired before listing:**
    - `ms-caring-for-an-adult-relative` — A STOCK USED AS A FLOW (AUTHORING.md §5.1 trap 2). The NAC/AARP figures are the age of people who currently ARE caregivers, not the age at which anyone starts or does caregiving. timing.window {49.4, 51} is additionally not a range at all: 49.4 is the mean and 51.0 the median of the same distribution, mashed together as if they were its two ends. select.ts places the record on the spine only in the years inside window, so a person caring for a parent at 35 or at 70 does not appear on the timeline anywhere. The hedge lives only in population ("not the age at which caregiving starts"), which §5.1 trap 3 says does not rescue the record - and the batch's own ms-caregiving-onset-age concedes no onset source was found, so the batch asserts the age and admits it does not have it in the same file.
        - found on re-fetch: Excerpt confirmed verbatim (p.10): "On average, caregivers of adults are 49.4 years old, with a median age of 51.0 years (see Figure 3)." Figure 3 on that same page: 18-34 = 24%, 35-49 = 23%, 50-64 = 35%, 65-74 = 12%, plus 75+. A window of 49.4-51 excludes roughly 90% of the caregivers the source counted.
        - fix: Drop timing from this record and merge it into ms-caregiving-onset-age as researchRequired (windowInWords, no digits). If a timing is wanted, re-encode as measure "share-at-age" over the report's actual age extent, which needs a verbatim sentence for the Figure 3 bands - "More than 4 in 10 are ages 18 to 49 (46 percent), while over half are ages 50 and over" is on the same page and is 22 words.
    - `ms-first-birth-mean-age` — timing.window {24.8, 28.5} is the spread between two county-group MEANS, not the outer extent of maternal age, and it is contradicted by the very NVSR report cited. It renders on the spine as the years in which becoming a parent happens and in windowText as a "seen" range. On a record flagged sensitivity "fertility", this is the §6 tone failure the guide names: a reader having a first child at 21 or at 34 is rendered outside the band on a page about her body. population discloses the construction, which §5.1 trap 3 says is not a rescue.
        - found on re-fetch: Same PDF, p.3, confirmed verbatim: "The percentage of first births to mothers younger than 25 declined by 26% for mothers younger than 20 (from 11.8% to 8.7%)" and "25% for mothers age 35 and older (from 10.0% to 12.5%)". So in 2023 roughly one in five first births fell outside 24.8-28.5 at the tails alone, plus 25.1% at ages 30-34.
        - fix: Keep typical {27.5, 27.5} with measure "mean" and remove window, or source a real outer extent. The urbanicity source belongs in the drawer as a variation note, not as the window; the between-group means can stay in whatChanges in words.
    - `ms-death-of-a-spouse-later-life` — INVENTED TIMING FROM SURVEY AGE UNIVERSES (§5.1 traps 1 and 2). No cited source prints an age at which the death of a spouse occurs. 75 is the lower edge of the Census denominator ("those 75 years or older"), 65 and 85 are the edges of the ACS age groups in Older Americans 2024, and "age 85 and over" is an open-ended top category truncated to 85 to make an upper bound. The digits therefore appear in the excerpts (rule A passes literally) while the claim they are used to make - measured in select.ts as "commonly 75-85 (seen 65-85)" - is made by no source. It also collapses the largest divergence in its own evidence into one band: 58% of women versus 28% of men at 75+, and 67.6% of women widowed at 85+.
        - found on re-fetch: Both excerpts verified verbatim: Census SIPP story - "Among those 75 years or older who had ever married, 58% of women and 28% of men had experienced the death of a spouse"; Older Americans 2024 Indicator 3 - "In 2022, nearly two-thirds of people ages 65-74 were married, whereas approximately one-tenth of people in this age group were widowed." Neither sentence is about when widowhood happens.
        - fix: Set researchRequired:true with windowInWords, or keep measure "share-at-age" with no window/typical spanning ages the sources never measure. If the record stays, the sex divergence needs a bySex block (both sources state what they measured) rather than a sentence in whatChanges.
    - `ms-death-of-a-spouse-later-life` — A RATE IN A PROSE FIELD. population reads "the share who are widowed is around one in ten in the youngest of these groups and is the larger share by the oldest". AUTHORING.md §4 is categorical: a percentage belongs in Source.excerpt "and nowhere else"; what a surface carries where a proportion matters is a band from the published table (most / about half / many / some / few). "Around one in ten" is the source's 10% restated in words, and "the larger share" restates "just over half". The digit lint does not catch it because it is spelled out, which is exactly why this is a human-read check.
        - found on re-fetch: content/timeline/schema.ts BAND_TABLE and AUTHORING.md §4 ("Rates never render on a surface"). The rate being restated is the excerpt's own "approximately one-tenth of people in this age group were widowed".
        - fix: Rewrite with band vocabulary: one-tenth falls in the "some" band (BAND_TABLE.some = 0.05) and "just over half" in "about half". The figures stay in the excerpts and render in the drawer.
    - `ms-becoming-a-grandparent` — Two different measures fused into one range, and a window with no lower tail. window.from = 50 is the MEAN age at first grandchild, so the outer extent begins at the average - the record asserts nobody becomes a grandparent before 50 while its own source says the youngest are about 38 and its own whatChanges says the survey's age floor truncates the young end. window.to = 65 comes from a cumulative-share sentence, which is the "most-by" measure, while the record declares measure "mean". A grandparent at 44 is placed nowhere on the spine.
        - found on re-fetch: Both AARP excerpts confirmed verbatim on p.3 of the PDF: "The youngest grandparents are about 38 years of age, with the average age at first grandchild being 50 (an increase of two years since 2011)." and "By age 65, almost all (96%) are grandparents." Methodology p.36: "Qualifications: Ages 38 or older; have 1 grandchild".
        - fix: Keep typical {50, 50} with measure "mean" and drop window (no outer extent is sourced), or split into a second record with measure "most-by" for the by-65 figure, which select.ts already renders in the source's own sentence shape ("most by 65").
    - `ms-first-marriage-median-age` — A MEDIAN ENCODED AS A BAND. AUTHORING.md §5 is explicit: "A median -> typical: {from: m, to: m} with measure: 'median'". Here typical is {28.4, 30.8} - the gap between the women's median and the men's median - and window is set to the identical pair. Because typical.from !== typical.to, select.ts windowText skips the median branch entirely and renders "commonly 28.4-30.8", i.e. a common band, which is precisely what a median does not say; and because window equals typical there is no "(seen ...)" to widen it. The two medians are already carried correctly and separately in the bySex block, so the top-level span adds nothing but the false band.
        - found on re-fetch: Excerpt confirmed verbatim in the Dec 2 2025 release "Census Bureau Releases New Estimates on America's Families and Living Arrangements": "The estimated median age at first marriage increased to 30.8 for men and 28.4 for women, up from ages 23.5 and 21.1, respectively, in 1975." The sentence supports two medians and no range.
        - fix: Let bySex carry the divergence and give the top level either a single sourced median as typical {m, m} with measure "median", or no timing span at all. window must not be a copy of typical.
- **Advisory findings (3):**
    - `src-aarp-grandparents-by-sixty-five` — The source's measures field claims "Share of adults who are grandparents by a given age", but this survey cannot produce that number: its base is 2,654 people who already have a grandchild and are 38 or older, and the 96% sentence in the executive summary carries no footnote (footnotes 1 and 2 on that line attach to the 56-to-70-million and the one-in-five-age-65 claims). The excerpt is genuinely on the page, so it is not an invented number, but it is an uncited assertion in a secondary source being used to set a timeline endpoint.
    - `src-aarp-grandparents-average-age` — Title mismatch. The batch titles both AARP sources "2019 AARP Grandparenting Study"; the document's own title page reads "2018 Grandparents Today National Survey". publicationYear 2019 and dataYear 2018 are both correct (fieldwork August 20 to September 4, 2018), so only the title string is off.
    - `ms-caregiving-onset-age` — Two smaller things on the unsourced record. windowInWords restates the research gap ("as distinct from the average age of everyone currently caregiving") rather than giving the shape of the claim in words, which is what §4.6 asks for and what renders in place of the digits. Separately, this record and ms-caring-for-an-adult-relative are about taking on care of an older relative and carry no sensitivity, careNote or /topics/health route; the subject is the caregiver's role rather than a body, so the compiler is satisfied, but it sits on the edge of the §6 health-decline segment and is worth a human decision rather than a silent default.
- **Repair pass:** verdict `clean`.

Assumption worth flagging: the verifier's fix for the marriage record ("no timing span at all") is not shippable as written, because tests/timeline-gates.ts T-1 (line ~358) fails any non-researchRequired record with no timing; I resolved it by AUTHORING.md §8 ("if a record is trying to say two things, it is two records") and split it into a women's and a men's record, each carrying its own median as typical {m, m}. Coverage is unaffected: re-running a year-by-year coverage pass over all eighteen batches gives the same empty-year set with and without this batch's timings.

        - found on re-fetch: Pew aging report, p.27: "Most Americans don't have a will or a living will until they are in their 70s." The record's own excerpt for the sixties gives 46% with a will and 44% with a directive — a minority, not "common". The chart bands run 15 / 20 / 32 / 46 / 66 / 81 across ages 18-39 through 80+.

The typical band should not be described as beginning in the sixties.

        - found on re-fetch: p2-inner-civic.json line 245: "Writing a will, and naming someone to make medical decisions if you cannot, is something most people report doing later in adulthood rather than early." Full-file grep for \byou\b|\byour\b returns this line and no other.
        - fix: Rewrite in the third person, e.g. "...and naming someone to make medical decisions on a person's behalf when they cannot...".

The record's only turnout excerpt names a single band as highest. The article's second endpoint sentence (18-24 lowest) is not excerpted, and even both endpoints together do not establish monotonicity.
        - found on re-fetch: src-usafacts-turnout-by-age excerpt in full: "In 2024, 74.7% of voters ages 65 or older voted, the highest rate among all age groups." The record's other source (src-census-p20590-eligible) is only the eligibility footnote. I searched the P20-590 PDF text for turnout rates by age and found only composition/share language ("Those ages 45-64 were the most predominant age group among voters... 33.3 percent") — shares, not rates, as the author himself noted.
        - fix: Either add an excerpt establishing the trend (Census Table A-9 rows, or the USAFacts sentence "Less than half of voters ages 18 to 24 voted, and were the least likely age group to vote in 2024") or reduce label and whatChanges to what one excerpt supports: the oldest published band reports the highest rate. Delete "climbs across adulthood".
    - `ms-voter-turnout-highest-in-later-life` — CLAIM EXCEEDS SOURCE (rule B), denominator. The record's population field asserts the rate is measured over "citizens eligible to vote", but the cited article states its Census figures are a percent of total population, not a citizen or registered base. The record silently upgrades a percent-of-population into a turnout rate among eligible citizens.
        - found on re-fetch: USAFacts article (re-fetched): "the Census Bureau only provides this data as a percent of total population, not of registered voters." The excerpt's own wording, "74.7% of voters ages 65 or older voted", is circular and states no denominator. Separately, Census Table A-9 puts 2024 turnout for 65-and-over at 71.7 percent, so 74.7 is not the citizen-base figure either — a discrepancy the author noticed and left unresolved.
        - fix: Replace the USAFacts source with the Census Table A-9 figure and state its denominator, or rewrite population to say what USAFacts actually measures. Do not leave a population field naming a base the source disclaims.
    - `ms-formal-volunteering-common-ages` — CLAIM EXCEEDS SOURCE (rule B). The record's typical band is 45-54, but the source names 16-17 as the highest rate of all age groups, with 45-54 second. Encoding the runner-up as the timeline's typical peak inverts the source's own ranking, and the load-bearing output of this record is the typical band, not the label prose that mentions both.

        - fix: Set typical to 16-17 (the source's actual peak), or split into two records, or drop typical and carry both high points in windowInWords. As written the timeline will surface 45-54 as the peak age for volunteering, which the source contradicts.
    - `ms-formal-volunteering-common-ages` — CLAIM EXCEEDS SOURCE (rule B). window.to = 54 asserts an upper bound on formal volunteering that the source never gives. 54 is the upper edge of the second-ranked band, not a ceiling; the CEV supplement covers people age 16 and over with no top age, so the record implies volunteering stops after 54.

The story's own title is "...More Than Half of People Age 16 and Over Helped Neighbors, 23% Formally Volunteered" — an open-ended 16-and-over universe.
        - fix: Do not close the window at 54. Either treat this as a two-point record with no continuous window, or close the window at the timeline ceiling with a notes line as the other five records do (subject to the resolution of the 100 finding).

  > File written to F:/Programming/The Guidebook to Life/tgtl-claude-5.0/content/timeline/batches/p2-inner-civic.json. Ran a local validator over it: no digits in any prose field, no normative phrases from the banned list, no loss-tier word on any record except the one carrying sensitivity "dying", all readRef/alsoRead routes valid, every typical sits inside its window, every excerpt is 25 words or fewer and verbatim, and every timing figure appears inside one of that record's own excerpts EXCEPT the number 100 (see below).
  > 
  > SOURCED (6 records): (1) voting age eighteen — 26th Amendment text from archives.gov plus the Census P20-590 numeral definition of eligibility; (2) reported voter turnout highest in the oldest band, window 18-100 typical 65-100; (3) formal volunteering most common at 16-17 and 45-54, window 16-54 typical 45-54; (4) daily leisure and sports hours lowest at 35-44 and highest at 75+, window 35-100 typical 75-100; (5) religious identity/prayer/attendance gap between ages 18-24 and 74+, window 18-100 typical 74-100; (6) will and advance directive from the sixties onward, window 60-100 typical 70-100, marked sensitivity "dying" with readRef /situations/a-death and alsoRead /situations/grief.
  > 
  > THE ONE THING A VERIFIER WILL FLAG, stated plainly: five records close their window at 100, and 100 appears in no excerpt. Every one of those sources reports an open-ended top band ("75 and over", "65 or older", "74 and older", "70s"/"80s or older"). There is no measured upper age to cite. I used 100 as this timeline's declared ceiling rather than invent a closed band, and each affected record carries a `notes` line saying the source's top band is open-ended and the window closes at the top of the timeline, not at a measured age. If the executor prefers, the alternative is to collapse those windows to the band's lower bound only, which would lose all coverage above age seventy-five.
  > 
  > Second assumption worth naming: the 26th Amendment writes the age as "eighteen", not "18", so the numeral 18 on that record is sourced from the Census P20-590 footnote ("citizens in the civilian population who are 18 years old or older"), which is why that record carries two sources.
  > 
  > Third: the turnout rate itself (74.7% for ages 65+) comes from USAFacts, which names the Census Bureau as its source. I could not find a .gov narrative sentence giving turnout RATES by age — the P20-590 and P20-586 reports discuss voter SHARES (composition), which is a different quantity and which I deliberately did not use, since "voters 65 and older made up 28% of voters" is not a turnout rate. Census Table A-9 (hst_vote09 / a9.xlsx) does carry the rates by age (65 and over: 71.7 in 2024) but it is a spreadsheet a re-fetching verifier is unlikely to be able to read, so I left it out rather than build an excerpt out of table cells. The USAFacts record is not sole-sourced.
  > 
  > Fourth: the volunteering figures are from the 2021 CEV supplement (published by Census in January 2023), a pandemic year the publisher itself flags as a period of decline. Newer AmeriCorps 2023 CEV material exists but its PDF yielded no extractable text and no by-age sentence could be read. The record carries a `notes` line saying so. Note the honest shape here: the highest rate of ALL age groups was among 16-17 year olds, with 45-54 second — I encoded both rather than presenting the adult peak as if it were the overall peak.
  > 
  > COULD NOT SOURCE (2 researchRequired): (a) life satisfaction / sense of purpose across adulthood — the "midlife dip" is widely described but no source I fetched gave a verbatim sentence naming the ages at which it turns; researchNote names BRFSS, HRS and GSS as the fetch targets. (b) organized civic and community participation before voting age — the CEV supplement's published age bands appear to start in the teenage years, so ages below roughly sixteen may not be measurable from that instrument at all; researchNote names NCES NHES as the likelier source. These two leave ages 0-15 uncovered by this batch, which other batches will need to carry.

  > All 9 source excerpts were re-fetched and confirmed VERBATIM on the live page, all are <=25 words, all publisher/publicationYear/dataYear values check out (one partial: db54 omits its 2004 survey), and every number appearing in a "timing" field does appear in an excerpt of that record's own sources — so there are no invented numbers and no dead URLs. The defects are in the inference layer, not the citation layer. Six blocking findings: (1) ms-living-alone-later-life is built entirely on a WOMEN-ONLY figure while its population field says "adults" — a conditional sub-case presented as the headline; (2) the same record asserts a women-vs-men comparison the Census page does not report at all (I checked specifically: no men living-alone percentage exists on that page); (3) the same record addresses the reader ("letting someone know you got home") outside "heard"; (4) ms-advance-directives-later-life collapses an open band ("85 years and over") plus an odds contrast inside a long-term-care universe into typical = exactly 85, and takes its window floor of 65 from the comparison group's ceiling ("under age 65"), an age at which the source shows nothing happening; (5) ms-required-minimum-distributions collapses a condition-varying rule into one exact age while its own population field names the group the exception applies to (workplace-plan owners still working may delay; the first RMD may also be delayed to April 1 of the next year — both qualifiers sit in the same IRS answer the excerpt is cut from); (6) ms-social-security-claiming-window encodes a legal PERMISSION span (62-70) as "typical", asserting a behavioral distribution the CFPB page never measures — the author's own note concedes no claiming-age data was obtainable; (7) ms-work-in-later-life declares measure "share-at-age" but neither of its excerpts contains any share at any age — both are projected-growth sentences, and the participation rates (8.9% / 11.7%) were cut out of the excerpt. Rules D, E, F, G, I, J, M, O are clean: no normative language, no digits in any prose field, no crisis content, loss-tier words confined to the two "dying" records, all readRefs in the allowed set, every "typical" nested inside its "window", no cultural-expectation records.

- **BLOCKING findings (7) — all repaired before listing:**
    - `ms-living-alone-later-life` — Rule B (conditional sub-case presented as the headline figure). The record's only source figures are for WOMEN — "about 27% of women ages 65 to 74 and 43% of those age 75 or older lived alone" — but the population field reads "adults counted in the Current Population Survey and reported in broad older age bands" and the label says "Living alone becomes a common household arrangement in later life" with no sex qualifier anywhere. The excerpt's leading clause "Among older adults," makes the women-only restriction easy to lose entirely. The window 65-75 is therefore an all-adults window resting on a women-only measurement.

Page (May 30, 2024, CPS 2022) reads: "Among older adults, about 27% of women ages 65 to 74 and 43% of those age 75 or older lived alone (Figure 2). In the age 75 and older group, men (67%) were nearly twice as likely as women (35%) to live with a spouse..." Excerpt confirmed verbatim; the figures are women-only.
        - fix: Either restrict the record to women in population and label, or swap the excerpt for the page's all-adults sentence — "Almost 1 in 10 young adults ages 18-34 and nearly 3 in 10 adults 65 or older lived alone in 2022" — which supports an all-adults claim but only gives a 65-or-older open band, so the window would have to be re-derived from it.
    - `ms-living-alone-later-life` — Rule B (claim exceeds source). whatChanges asserts living alone "is more common among women than among men counted in the same bands." The Census page reports NO living-alone percentage for men in any band. The only men/women contrast on the page is about living WITH A SPOUSE, which is a different measure; deriving the living-alone comparison from it is arithmetic the source never prints.

Result: only two — "nearly 3 in 10 adults 65 or older" (all adults) and the 27%/43% women figures. Confirmed: "The page does not report the percentage of men ages 65-74 or 75+ who lived alone."

    - `ms-advance-directives-later-life` — Rules C and B. (a) "typical": {from: 85, to: 85} collapses an OPEN band ("aged 85 years and over") into one exact age; the source publishes no age 85 event and no closed band ending at 85. (b) The window floor of 65 is taken from the comparison group's ceiling ("those under age 65") — 65 is the top of the reference band, not an age at which the source shows anything occurring. (c) The measure is "share-at-age" but the excerpt carries no share at all: it is an odds contrast, and the actual percentages (41% vs 17% home health; 77% vs 36% nursing home) were cut out of the excerpt. (d) The whole comparison lives inside a universe of people already receiving long-term care, so it is a stock inside a narrow universe, placed on a general-life timeline at 65-85.

Full sentence: "Among home health care and nursing home populations, those aged 85 years and over were more than twice as likely as those under age 65 to have an AD—41% compared with 17%". Excerpt confirmed verbatim but truncated before the shares. Population studied: home health care patients, nursing home residents, discharged hospice patients.

Either re-source from a general-population advance-directive study that reports a share at an age, or convert this record to windowInWords / researchRequired the way ms-hospice-and-palliative-availability was handled — the same objection the author raised against the PMC odds finding applies to this NCHS sentence.
    - `ms-required-minimum-distributions` — Rule C (condition-varying thing collapsed into one exact age). timing is window 73-73 and typical 73-73, and whatChanges states absolutely that "A minimum amount has to come out of the account for that year." The IRS answer the excerpt is cut from carries two qualifiers that change the age: the first RMD may be delayed to April 1 of the FOLLOWING year, and workplace-retirement-plan participants may delay RMDs until the year they retire (unless a 5% owner). The record's own population field explicitly includes "workplace retirement plan accounts" — i.e. it names the group for whom 73 is not the operative age.

Excerpt confirmed verbatim. Q3 continues: "However, you can delay taking the first RMD until April 1 of the following year..." and the page separately notes workplace-plan participants may delay until the year they retire, plus a 403(b) pre-1987 age-75 rule.
        - fix: Either narrow population to traditional IRA owners (for whom 73 is unconditional) and drop the workplace-plan clause, or keep the broader population and state the still-working exception in whatChanges — currently the condition is invisible.
    - `ms-social-security-claiming-window` — Rule B (claim exceeds source). The CFPB page states a PERMISSION span — a worker is allowed to claim between 62 and 70. The record encodes that identical span as "typical": {from: 62, to: 70}, which asserts that claiming typically occurs across the whole span. No source measures claiming behavior; the author's own note concedes the observed claiming-age distribution could not be obtained (ssa.gov and crsreports 403'd). A legal permission range is being presented as a behavioral distribution. The label reinforces this: "benefits are claimed somewhere inside a span of years" is a behavioral sentence attached to measure "legal-rule".
        - found on re-fetch: Re-fetched https://www.consumerfinance.gov/consumer-tools/retirement/before-you-claim/ (Page last modified Jan. 29, 2026). Excerpt confirmed verbatim in the "Consider whether you can wait to claim" section; the page states permission only and attributes the rules to the Social Security Administration.
        - fix: Remove "typical" from this record (leave the rule window 62-70 only), and reword the label to a permission statement, e.g. "Social Security retirement benefits can first be claimed inside a span of years." Restore a "typical" only if the SSA claiming-age distribution is obtained.
    - `ms-work-in-later-life` — Rule B / measure-evidence mismatch. measure is "share-at-age" and evidence is "evidence-informed", but NEITHER of this record's two excerpts contains a share at any age. Both are projection sentences about growth rates. The record's substantive claim — that paid work continues for some share of people at 65-75 — has no figure behind it; the window boundaries 65 and 75 are lifted purely from band LABELS inside sentences about projected growth. The actual participation levels (8.9% in 2020 rising to 11.7% by 2030) were cut out of the BLS excerpt.
        - found on re-fetch: Re-fetched https://www.bls.gov/opub/ted/2021/... (Nov 4, 2021; base 2020, projected 2030): full sentence is "The only age group whose labor force participation rate is projected to rise are people age 75 and older, from 8.9 percent in 2020 to 11.7 percent by 2030." Re-fetched https://www.bls.gov/careeroutlook/2017/article/older-workers.htm (May 2017, 2014-24 projections): "...the 65- to 74-year-old and 75-and-older age groups are projected to have faster rates of labor force growth annually than any other age groups." Both excerpts verbatim; neither contains a share at an age.
        - fix: Add the BLS civilian labor force participation rate table (https://www.bls.gov/emp/tables/civilian-labor-force-participation-rate.htm — the author fetched it but did not cite it) with an excerpt carrying the 65-74 and 75+ participation rates, or extend the TED excerpt to include "from 8.9 percent in 2020 to 11.7 percent by 2030" (still under 25 words) so a share exists in evidence.
- **Advisory findings (5):**
    - `src-nchs-advance-directives-ltc` — dataYear is 2007, but the brief draws on TWO surveys: the 2004 National Nursing Home Survey and the 2007 National Home and Hospice Care Survey. The nursing-home half of the very contrast being cited (77% vs 36%) is 2004 data, so the declared dataYear understates the age of the evidence by three years.
    - `src-cfpb-claiming-age` — kind is "statute-or-agency-rule", but this is a CFPB consumer education tool restating another agency's rules, not the issuing agency's rule text. The measures field admits as much ("restating the rules of the Social Security Administration"). A secondary restatement carrying the weight of a rule source.
    - `src-irs-rmd-faq` — Rule K ambiguity worth an executor decision: this excerpt and src-cfpb-claiming-age's excerpt both address the reader directly ("You must take your first required minimum distribution...", "you are allowed to claim your benefits..."). Rule K exempts only "heard". I am NOT scoring these blocking because the stated rationale for the "heard" exemption is that it is quotation, and an excerpt is equally quotation — but if the executor reads rule K as applying to every field but "heard", these two excerpts violate it, and rendered in a reader-facing surface they read as direct financial instruction.
    - `ms-oldest-ages` — Rule H judgement call the author flagged. The record carries no sensitivity, but its label — "Reaching the oldest ages is uncommon" — is a statement about survival, which a reader in their eighties can read as a countdown. It also sits in lane body-health with readRef /topics/health but no careNote.
    - `ms-residential-care-community` — Rule B stock/flow softness. The source measures the age composition of people currently LIVING in residential care; the timing (window 65-85, typical 75-85) places it on a timeline where it reads as when a move happens, and whatChanges uses transition language — "A household of one's own is exchanged for a room or apartment inside a community." The source measures no age at move-in.   > File: F:/Programming/The Guidebook to Life/tgtl-claude-5.0/content/timeline/batches/p2-late-life-transitions.json (valid JSON, verified by parse).

  > FIGURE AUDIT — every number in every timing field appears inside an excerpt of that record's own sources: living-alone 65/75 (Census 65-74, 75-or-older); residential-care 65/75/85 (NCHS db506 lists 85, 75-84, 65-74); advance-directives 65/85 (NCHS db54); claiming 62/70 (CFPB); RMD 73 (IRS); work 65/75 (BLS Career Outlook "65- to 74-year-old and 75-and-older"); oldest-ages 85/99/100 (Census centenarian release: "population ages 85 to 99" and "reached the age of 100").   > 
  > HONEST LIMITS:
  > 1. Open-ended top bands. Census living-alone, BLS labor force, NCHS residential care and NCHS advance directives all publish an open final band ("75 or older", "85 and over"). I did NOT stretch those windows to 100. Each window stops where the open band begins, and each `population` field says so in words. Only ms-oldest-ages reaches 100, and only because the Census centenarian release supplies a genuinely closed band (85 to 99) plus an explicit 100.
  > 2. Social Security claiming ages ACTUALLY CHOSEN could not be sourced — ssa.gov returned 403 to this fetcher on every route tried (program explainer, OACT beneficiary-by-age table, Annual Statistical Supplement 6.B, retirement planner), and crsreports.congress.gov also 403'd. I therefore encoded the RULE window (earliest 62, latest 70) from CFPB, which names SSA as its source, rather than a median or modal claiming age. A verifier with SSA access should replace or supplement this with the observed claiming-age distribution.
  > 3. Advance directives: the only .gov source giving an age contrast is NCHS Data Brief 54 (published 2011; 2004 NNHS / 2007 NHHCS). Stale, and its universe is people already receiving long-term care, not the general population — the record's `population` states this. I deliberately did NOT use the widely-cited PMC finding that "middle old (75-84) and oldest old (85+) had nearly double the odds vs young old (65-74)", because that is a within-universe odds comparison, i.e. exactly the age-universe trap.
  > 4. Hospice/palliative care encoded as researchRequired, per instruction — it is a covered service with no age of its own, and the only age-banded CMS figures are shares among beneficiaries who died, which is a decedent universe, not an age at which something happens. It still carries sensitivity "dying", careNote, readRef /situations/a-death and alsoRead /situations/grief; if the schema rejects those keys on a researchRequired record, the executor should keep the routes and drop the record rather than drop the routes.
  > 5. Judgement call flagged: ms-oldest-ages carries no `sensitivity`. It is a demographic count of people at the oldest ages, not health decline, fertility or dying, and none of its prose touches bodies or decline. If the reviewer wants it treated as sensitive, "health-decline" plus a careNote is the change.
  > 6. No record uses a percentage anywhere outside a Source.excerpt; no prose field in any record contains a digit (checked programmatically); loss-tier words appear only on the two records whose sensitivity is "dying".

### `p2-later-health`

- **Verifier verdict:** `repairs-required` — re-fetched 12 source(s), confirmed 12.

  > All twelve URLs re-fetched successfully; every excerpt appears character-for-character on its page, every excerpt is under twenty-five words and contains its figure, and publisher/publicationYear/dataYear/measures match what the pages state. Every literal in every timing block does appear inside an excerpt belonging to that record's own sources, so there are no invented digits. The mechanical gates all pass: no digit in any prose field, no normative-lint hit, no crisis-tier term, no loss-tier term outside a health-decline record, typical inside window everywhere, readRef /topics/health resolves in content/routes.ts, retrievedOn inside the build window, whatChanges within three lines and forty words. The researchRequired handling of remaining life expectancy at sixty-five is correct and is the strongest thing in the batch: it refuses the arithmetic AUTHORING 5.1 trap 4 forbids and names the source text that would resolve it. Five blocking defects remain, all of the same family: a number that is genuinely on the page has been given a meaning the page does not give it. The life-expectancy window is a male/female average gap drawn as a range of ages at death. The hearing record splices a thirty-five-decibel "disabling" series onto a twenty-five-decibel "any loss" series and calls the result one rise. The chronic-conditions record anchors its upper endpoint to an excerpt that reports a count distribution and never reports the two-or-more share the record claims, and marks its least-prevalent band as the denser zone. The daily-activities record asserts an unsourced comparison against paid services using a band-table word. None of these are catchable by the compiler; all of them change what a reader sees on the spine.

- **BLOCKING findings (5) — all repaired before listing:**
    - `ms-life-expectancy-at-birth` — timing.window {from: 75.8, to: 81.1} is not a window. 75.8 is the male life expectancy at birth and 81.1 is the female value; the band is the gap between two sex-specific averages, not the extent of anything. Schema documents window as "the outer extent, including the tails" of when a thing commonly happens, so this renders on the spine as if lives commonly end between roughly seventy-six and eighty-one. No source in the batch states any range of ages at death, and the record's own whatChanges[2] contradicts the band it draws: "Actual lifespans spread very widely around the average." The schema has a dedicated construct for exactly this case (bySex: {female: Timing, male: Timing, measures, sources}) which was not used. Secondary: AgeRange is documented as "an inclusive age range in whole or half years" and 75.8 / 81.1 / 78.4 are neither.
        - found on re-fetch: Source excerpts (both confirmed verbatim on https://www.cdc.gov/nchs/products/databriefs/db521.htm): "For males, life expectancy increased 1.0 year from 74.8 in 2022 to 75.8 in 2023." and "For females, life expectancy increased 0.9 year from 80.2 in 2022 to 81.1 in 2023." Neither sentence, nor the total-population sentence, describes a range of ages at which people die.
        - fix: Drop window entirely and carry timing {typical: {from: 78.4, to: 78.4}} with measure "mean" (the schema's documented encoding for a mean). Move the male and female values into bySex with measures naming what the brief measured and sources set to the two sex-specific source ids. If a window is wanted on the spine, it needs a source that itself prints a distribution of ages at death, and there is none in this batch.
    - `ms-age-related-hearing-loss` — The record fuses two incommensurable prevalence series into one rising claim. NIDCD reports "disabling hearing loss", defined on that page as thirty-five decibels or more in the better ear. The JAMA/NHATS study reports any hearing loss at a better-ear pure-tone average above twenty-five decibels, and its 96.2% at ninety-plus is 21.0% mild plus 53.4% moderate plus 21.8% severe — over a fifth of that figure is mild loss the NIDCD series excludes by definition. whatChanges[1] presents the two as a single series: "The share of people affected rises with each older age band, from a small minority in midlife to nearly everyone in the oldest bands measured." The word "affected" changes meaning mid-sentence. Neither the population field nor src-jama-hearing-oldest-old.measures ("Audiometrically measured hearing loss prevalence by age stratum...") states the threshold, so the evidence drawer will show five percent and ninety-six percent side by side with nothing telling the reader they count different things. The sibling record ms-multiple-chronic-conditions does disclose its survey mixing in whatChanges[2]; this one does not.
        - found on re-fetch: Confirmed on https://www.nidcd.nih.gov/health/statistics/quick-statistics-hearing: "hearing loss of 35 decibels or more in the better ear, the level at which adults could generally benefit from hearing aids". Confirmed on https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10383002/: categories are no hearing loss (<=25 dBHL), mild (26-40), moderate (41-60), severe (>60); the 90-plus breakdown is 3.8% normal, 21.0% mild, 53.4% moderate, 21.8% severe or worse.
        - fix: Either split into two records (disabling hearing loss from NIDCD; measured hearing loss among older Medicare beneficiaries from NHATS), or keep one record and (a) put the decibel threshold into each source's measures field so the drawer discloses it, and (b) rewrite whatChanges[1] so it does not assert one continuous rise across the two definitions — e.g. state each series separately in the population field and drop the spliced "from a small minority ... to nearly everyone" arc.
    - `ms-multiple-chronic-conditions` — The upper endpoint of the window (85) is anchored to src-nchs-chronic-conditions-oldest, whose excerpt reports a distribution of condition counts and never reports a two-or-more share. The record's population field asserts of both sources: "The counts are of people carrying two or more chronic conditions at the same time, in the terms each survey uses." That is true of the PCD source and false of the NCHS source, whose own measures field correctly says "Number of selected chronic conditions reported". Reading the two-or-more share out of that excerpt requires subtracting the none and one categories from a hundred — arithmetic the source does not print, which is the operation AUTHORING 5.1 trap 4 forbids. Rule A passes only on the literal "85"; the claim attached to that endpoint is not in the excerpt.
        - found on re-fetch: src-nchs-chronic-conditions-oldest excerpt, confirmed verbatim on https://www.cdc.gov/nchs/data/hestat/hestat105.htm: "Among adults age 85 and older, 7.4% reported none of the selected chronic conditions, 12.1% reported one condition, 20.8% reported two conditions". The full page sentence continues "22.3% reported three conditions, and 37.3% reported four or more conditions" — the two-or-more figure exists only as a sum the page never states.
        - fix: Either replace the excerpt with a sentence from an NCHS page that itself prints a two-or-more share for the oldest band, or narrow the record's population field to say the oldest band's source reports a distribution of condition counts rather than a two-or-more count, and stop describing both sources with one sentence. If neither is available, pull the 85 endpoint and let the window end where a sourced two-or-more figure ends.
    - `ms-multiple-chronic-conditions` — timing.typical {from: 65, to: 74} names the least-prevalent measured band as the denser zone. The schema defines typical as "the denser zone inside the window" and the compiler's own failure message calls it "the denser part of the window". The record's sources show prevalence rising with age, so 65-74 is the sparsest end of the 65-85 window, not the densest. The sibling record in the same batch, ms-help-with-daily-activities, uses typical {75, 85} — the higher-prevalence bands — over the identical window {65, 85}. The two records therefore use the same field to mean opposite things, which proves one of them is misencoded, and it is this one. On the spine the denser drawing lands on the wrong ages.
        - found on re-fetch: src-cdc-multimorbidity-older-adults, confirmed on https://www.cdc.gov/pcd/issues/2016/16_0174.htm, in its full sentence: "it was 64% (95% CI, 61%-66%) among participants aged 65 to 74 and 71% (95% CI, 68%-73%) among those aged 75 or older." Prevalence is higher, not lower, above 74.
        - fix: Set typical to the higher-prevalence end of the window so it matches the schema's definition and the sibling record's usage, or, if the intent was "the band where it first becomes common", drop typical entirely and let window carry the claim — the sources support a rising gradient, not a dense zone.
    - `ms-help-with-daily-activities` — whatChanges[1] reads "Most of that help comes first from family or friends in the home rather than from paid services." Neither source measures paid services, and neither compares informal to formal care, so the comparison is unsourced. Worse, "Most" is a published band-table word — BAND_TABLE.most is the 0.66 threshold and bandFor() maps a sourced proportion onto it — so this line renders a quantitative band on a surface for a proportion no source in the batch states. AUTHORING section 4 permits a band on a surface only where a sourced proportion sits behind it. Related mismatch in the same record: the label says "Needing another person's help", but the source that supplies the 65 and 85 endpoints measures something else — having received care at home from a friend or family member during the past twelve months, which includes short-term care after illness or surgery and is not a statement about needing help.
        - found on re-fetch: src-mmwr-home-care-family, confirmed verbatim on https://www.cdc.gov/mmwr/volumes/72/wr/mm7223a8.htm, full sentence: "The percentage of adults who received care during the past 12 months was similar among adults aged 18-44 years (9.8%) and 45-64 years (10.5%), then increased with age to 13.8% among those aged 65-74 years, 19.4% among those aged 75-84 years, and more than doubled to 39.8% among those aged >=85 years." No mention of paid services anywhere; src-mmwr-adl-help likewise reports only ADL and IADL help percentages.
        - fix: Delete whatChanges[1] or replace it with a line the sources support, e.g. that the care measured in the oldest band is care received at home from a friend or family member. Separately, extend the population field to say plainly that the age bands come from a past-twelve-months receipt-of-care measure rather than from a measure of needing help, so the label and the timing anchor are not read as the same thing.
- **Advisory findings (5):**
    - `ms-age-related-hearing-loss` — whatChanges[2] is entirely what-to-do-about-it: "Hearing aids, captions, and choosing quieter rooms are the routes people use; an audiologist assesses an individual." AUTHORING section 8 defines whatChanges as "What actually changes when this happens - not why it matters, not what to do about it." The compiler does not check this, so it will ship. The careNote already routes to an audiologist, so the line is also duplicative.
    - `ms-age-related-hearing-loss` — whatChanges[0] asserts an ordering of symptom onset - "Following speech in a noisy room usually gets harder before quiet conversation does" - that neither cited source states. The author's own urlsFetched list includes https://www.nidcd.nih.gov/health/age-related-hearing-loss, which does carry material of this kind, but that page is not among the batch's twelve sources and so is not attached to this record.
    - `ms-life-expectancy-at-birth` — Both life-expectancy records carry sensitivity "health-decline" and route only to /topics/health. Their subject is not decline in health, it is how long lives run; on the spine this is the timeline's dying material. The schema routes sensitivity "dying" to /situations/a-death and /situations/grief, named first. As encoded, the one band on the spine that marks the end of life offers a reader neither page. This is a judgment call rather than a rule break - the compiler's requirement is satisfied for the sensitivity the record declares - so it is flagged rather than blocked, but it is the classification most likely to be wrong here. The same applies to ms-remaining-years-at-sixty-five.
    - `ms-age-related-hearing-loss` — timing.window.from = 45 is a survey age-band boundary, not a measured onset. The excerpt supplying it reports five percent prevalence across ages 45-54 - the bottom of the "some" band in BAND_TABLE - so the window for a record labelled "becomes widespread" opens at the age where its own source shows the smallest measured share. This is the shape of AUTHORING 5.1 trap 1 (a survey's age universe is not a measured age), though the label's "becomes" reads as a process across the window rather than an assertion about its start, which is why it is advisory rather than blocking.
    - `src-nidcd-hearing-midlife` — dataYear is a single integer but the underlying data spans several survey years, and the batch silently resolves this by taking the last year. src-nidcd-hearing-midlife and src-nidcd-hearing-older carry dataYear 2020 for NHANES 2015-2020; src-cdc-multimorbidity-older-adults carries dataYear 2012 for NHANES 2005-2012. The convention is defensible and consistently applied, but it is unstated, and the reference-frame line promises "each figure shows its own data year".
- **Repair pass:** verdict `clean`.

- **Author's notes to the executor:**

  > File written to F:/Programming/The Guidebook to Life/tgtl-claude-5.0/content/timeline/batches/p2-later-health.json. Five sourced milestones plus one researchRequired; twelve sources, all .gov except one peer-reviewed JAMA Network Open study reached via PubMed Central. I ran a script over the finished file confirming: every from/to value in every timing block appears as a literal string inside an excerpt belonging to that record's own sources; every excerpt is at most twenty-five words; no digit appears in any label, population, careNote, windowInWords, measures or whatChanges line; no whatChanges line exceeds forty words; no forbidden normative phrase appears; typical sits inside window everywhere.
  > 
  > Life expectancy at sixty-five is the researchRequired record, deliberately. The NCHS brief prints the remaining years at that age but never prints the age that span reaches, and the instruction forbids computing it, so the record carries a researchNote naming exactly what source text would resolve it. Nothing was transformed anywhere else either.
  > 
  > Coverage: hearing loss runs from the mid forties to ninety, chronic conditions and help with daily activities each run from sixty-five to eighty-five, presbyopia from the mid forties to the mid sixties, and life expectancy at birth spans the male and female values. The assigned range from seventy-four to eighty-nine is therefore covered continuously, mainly by the hearing window. Nothing in this batch reaches past ninety: I could not find a .gov or peer-reviewed page that prints an age above ninety in prose for any later-life health measure, and every candidate agency source tops out at an open-ended "and older" band, which I refused to encode as a hundred because that upper bound appears in no excerpt. The years past ninety will need another batch or a life-table narrative that names those ages directly.
  > 
  > Two judgment calls worth flagging. First, measure "mean" on life expectancy at birth: the source does not use the word mean, but its own definitions section calls it the average number of years of life remaining, so mean is the honest fit and the population field says so in the source's terms. Second, the researchRequired record carries sensitivity and careNote even though the researchRequired template in the brief does not list those fields; the sensitive-record rule is release-blocking and this record is about later-life health, so I complied with the rule rather than the template. If the schema rejects extra fields there, those two keys are the ones to drop.
  > 
  > Also note the multiple-chronic-conditions record mixes two vintages: the younger band comes from a peer-reviewed CDC journal analysis of an older examination survey, the oldest band from a recent NCHS health e-stat. Different surveys count different condition lists, which is stated in the record's third whatChanges line rather than hidden.

No invented numbers: every digit in every timing field appears in an excerpt of that record's own sources. The repo compiler (tools/timeline-build-content.mjs, run under Node 22 with --experimental-strip-types) emits all 18 batches clean, so digits-in-prose, the normative wall, crisis/loss tiers, excerpt length, typical-inside-window, retrievedOn window, and readRef resolution all pass — the /map/credential-decision readRef is valid (content/routes.ts is the live inventory; route-inventory.json is the legacy 2.0 list). No record is sensitive-tier and none ought to be. THREE BLOCKING DEFECTS, all the same shape: a survey age universe or a reporting bracket written into timing.window, which schema.ts defines as "the outer extent, including the tails" — AUTHORING.md §5.1 trap 1. The test I applied consistently: does the quoted clause itself assert the encoded claim? For the BLS record it does ("most people have completed their schooling by age 25" IS a most-by statement), so that one is advisory. For the three below it does not — the clause states dataset coverage or a table caption, and the record turns that boundary into an extent. Worst of the three is the bachelor's record, whose own excerpt prints the tails it excludes (21.0% at 24-29, 15.5% at 30 or older) while the window says 23 to 23.

- **BLOCKING findings (3) — all repaired before listing:**
    - `ms-bachelors-degree-completed-by-early-twenties` — timing.window is {from: 23, to: 23}, but schema.ts defines window as "the outer extent, including the tails" — and this record's own excerpt prints those tails. The B&B:16/17 Table 1 rows quoted in src-nces-bb-age-completed-degree-requirements are "23 or younger 63.4 65.2 24–29 21.0 20.3 30 or older 15.5 14.5": roughly a third of bachelor's recipients complete degree requirements AFTER 23, and the top bucket is open-ended. The record therefore renders a spine window that its own cited source contradicts in the same JSON object. The degenerate-window convention used elsewhere (p2-child-development's most-by records) is not a precedent here: those sources publish no tail, this one publishes two. Rule B (claim exceeds source) and Rule C (a spread thing collapsed to one exact age).
        - found on re-fetch: Source excerpt in this file: "23 or younger 63.4 65.2 24–29 21.0 20.3 30 or older 15.5 14.5". Confirmed verbatim in the PDF (Table 1, "Age completed requirements for 2015–16 bachelor's degree"), extracted from https://nces.ed.gov/pubs2019/2019241.pdf. Record timing: {"window":{"from":23,"to":23},"typical":{"from":23,"to":23}}. whatChanges even states the contradiction in words: "some in the second half of the twenties and some at thirty or beyond."
        - fix: Keep typical {23,23} with measure most-by (that is what "23 or younger 63.4" supports) and either (a) widen window to {23,30} with population saying the oldest group is open-ended, or (b) drop window entirely so the record renders only the sourced most-by point. Do not leave a window whose upper edge is below two printed buckets of the same table.
    - `ms-living-in-a-parents-home-young-adults` — timing.window {18, 34} is taken from a table CAPTION — the Census Bureau's denominator for the AD-1 series — and rendered as the outer extent of the phenomenon. "Table AD-1. Young Adults, 18-34 Years Old, Living At Home" defines who is counted; it says nothing about the ages at which living in a parent's home starts or stops. Living in a parent's home has no lower extent of 18 (it is near-universal below 18) and no upper extent of 34; the same series would carry different numbers if Census defined young adults as 18-29. This is AUTHORING.md §5.1 trap 1 verbatim ("A survey's age universe is not a measured age") and Rule B bullet 1. The excerpt technically "contains the figure," but only because the figure IS the universe — the citation is circular.
        - found on re-fetch: src-census-ad1-young-adults-living-at-home excerpt: "Table AD-1. Young Adults, 18-34 Years Old, Living At Home: 1960 to Present" — confirmed verbatim on https://www.census.gov/data/tables/time-series/demo/families/adults.html (re-fetched; it is the table's title, and the sibling tables AD-2/AD-3 are captioned to 2023). Record timing: {"window":{"from":18,"to":34},"typical":{"from":18,"to":24}}.
        - fix: Drop the {18,34} window. For a share-at-age record the honest encoding is the bands where a share was actually quoted: typical {18,24} (majority arrangement) is defensible because the same story prints "More than half of adult men and women in that age group lived in a parental home"; the 25-34 share (17.8%) is the other quoted band. Either set window {18,34} aside and let the two quoted bands carry the record, or state in population that the outer edges are the Census reporting bracket and not an extent.
    - `ms-leaving-the-parental-home` — timing.window.from = 15 is the study's harmonized sample coverage / analytic restriction, not a measured lower extent of US home-leaving. The quoted clause asserts dataset coverage — "all include individuals aged 15 to 30 years old" — across four national panels (NLSY97, PAIRFAM, TYP, CFPS) spanning the US, Germany, Taiwan and China; the relative clause "which is the decisive period" is the authors' justification for that coverage, not a US result. The paper's own limitations section names it as a constraint: "the age range in the analysis was restricted to between 15 and 30." Applying the four-country lower bound as the US window's lower edge is Rule B bullet 1. The record's own label disagrees with its window — the label says "across the late teens and the twenties," while 15 is mid-teens. (The upper bound 30 is fine: it is independently supported by a US-specific result sentence.)
        - found on re-fetch: src-nauck-leaving-home-decisive-period excerpt: "all include individuals aged 15 to 30 years old, which is the decisive period for leaving the parental home" — confirmed verbatim in the PDF, in section 4.1 describing the four datasets. Same PDF, limitations: "Moreover, the age range in the analysis was restricted to between 15 and 30, and the measure of home-leaving was a composite of retrospective and panel...". Also confirmed verbatim: "The decline in living with parents was steeper in the United States, with most departures completed by age 25" and "the final result was almost 90% of the young adults leaving the parental home by age 30 in the United States and Germany". NLSY97 = birth cohorts 1980-1984, observation 1997-2014 (population and dataYear 2014 both check out).
        - fix: Either drop the lower edge and encode window {25,30} with typical {25,25} and measure most-by (the fallback the author already identified — both edges then rest on US-specific result sentences), or keep 15 only if population says plainly that the lower edge is the four-country studies' age coverage rather than a measured age. A hedge that only lives in population does not rescue the rendered window, so the first option is the safer repair.

BLS publishes no distribution behind "most people have completed their schooling by age 25" — it is an agency assertion justifying a denominator, and 25 is literally the reporting universe boundary. I did not call this blocking because, unlike the three above, the quoted clause does itself assert the encoded claim (a most-by statement at 25), so the record does not exceed its source. The same inference was applied to src-bls-attainment-reported-from-age-twenty-five (dataYear 2025).
    - `src-census-young-adults-in-parents-household` — The excerpt is a verbatim prefix but is cut mid-clause, ending on "parents" and dropping the possessive and the noun. It reads as a sentence fragment in the evidence drawer, and there was no length pressure — it is 14 words against a 25-word limit. Separately, this source's title carries a rate ("Estimated 17.8%...") outside an excerpt; the compiler does not digit-lint source titles, so this is not a build failure, but AUTHORING.md §4 says rates render only inside the evidence drawer's excerpt.
    - `ms-living-in-a-parents-home-young-adults` — measure is "share-at-age" ("share of people at that age") but the evidence for the 18-24 band that drives typical is modal, not a share: the Census story says living in a parent's home was the MOST COMMON arrangement, and the batch quotes only that sentence. The Measure enum contains "modal" ("most common single value") for exactly this.

(4) ms-leaving-the-parental-home — Nauck, Groepler & Yi 2017 (Demographic Research 36:38, US arm = NLSY97): window 15-30 from the authors' "decisive period" sentence, typical/most-by 25 from "most departures completed by age 25", upper bound corroborated by "almost 90% ... by age 30 in the United States".

- **Verifier verdict:** `repairs-required` — re-fetched 5 source(s), confirmed 5.

  > All five sources re-fetched independently and all five excerpts confirmed VERBATIM, character-for-character, including both CDC PDF excerpts (WebFetch returned binary; I extracted the text myself with pypdf and string-matched both sentences on page 3 of NHSR 146). Publisher, publicationYear, dataYear, URL liveness and word counts all check out; nothing 404s or redirects. Mechanical walls are clean: zero digits in any prose field, zero normative-lint hits (I ran the actual patterns from content/timeline/normative-lint.ts against every field), no reader address, no percentages outside excerpts, no loss-tier or crisis-tier vocabulary, all three records correctly flagged sensitivity "puberty" with careNote and readRef "/topics/health", typical contained by window everywhere, whatChanges within three lines and forty words, status/evidence/kind/lane/readRef all valid against schema.ts and route-inventory.json. The author's self-report was accurate and their disclosed interpretive call was real.
  > 
  > The batch nevertheless fails on ONE systematic defect that hits all three records: the "window" field. schema.ts defines window as "the outer extent, INCLUDING THE TAILS" and AUTHORING.md §5 as "the outer extent" with typical as "the denser zone inside it". All three records populate window with a band that is demonstrably NOT the outer extent, and in each case the record's own source contradicts it on the same page. On the two onset records, window simply duplicates the sourced TYPICAL range — and the NICHD page the range comes from states on that same page that puberty begins before age 8 in girls and before age 9 in boys (precocious) and later than the normal range (delayed), so 8-13 and 9-14 are not outer extents and the source says so explicitly. On the menarche record, window 10-14 is the author's own assembly of two cumulative-probability cutpoints, and NHSR 146 Table 2 prints the tails it excludes (cumulative probability 0.01 at age 8, 0.04 at 9, 0.10 at 10, 0.90 at 14, 0.96 at 15) — roughly one in ten before 10 and one in ten after 14. The qualification that the band does not cover every case lives only in "population", which is trap 3 of AUTHORING §5.1.
  > 
  > Each fix is a deletion, not new research: drop "window" and keep the sourced "typical". For menarche the author already named the correct fallback in their own notes.

- **BLOCKING findings (3) — all repaired before listing:**
    - `ms-puberty-onset-girls` — timing.window {from: 8, to: 13} asserts an outer extent the source contradicts. schema.ts defines `window` as "the outer extent, including the tails" and AUTHORING.md §5 as the outer extent with `typical` as "the denser zone inside it". Here window merely duplicates typical, so the record claims puberty in girls never begins outside 8-13. The NICHD page this range comes from says otherwise on the same page. The sourced sentence supports only a TYPICAL range (the source's own word is "typically"), which is already correctly encoded as typical with measure "typical-range". This is AUTHORING §5.1 trap 3: the hedge that the range describes a population lives only in `population`, while the typed field renders an absolute bound.
        - found on re-fetch: Source excerpt (confirmed verbatim on https://www.nichd.nih.gov/health/topics/puberty/conditioninfo, last reviewed 6/21/2021): "The physical changes that mark puberty typically begin in girls between ages 8 and 13 and in boys between ages 9 and 14." — the word is "typically". The SAME page states verbatim: "Precocious (meaning prematurely developed) puberty begins before age 8 for girls and before age 9 for boys." and "Delayed puberty is the term for a condition in which the body's timing for sexual maturity is later than the normal range of ages." So onset before 8 and after 13 both occur and the source names them. StatPearls (https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK470280/) likewise says "the normal onset" ranges 8 to 13, not the full extent.
        - fix: Delete `window` from timing, leaving `timing: { typical: { from: 8, to: 13 } }` with measure "typical-range". Timing in schema.ts requires only that at least one field be present, so typical alone is valid. Do not invent a wider window; neither source prints an outer extent.
    - `ms-puberty-onset-boys` — Identical defect. timing.window {from: 9, to: 14} duplicates the sourced typical range and asserts that puberty in boys never begins outside 9-14. The source supports a typical range only, and the same NICHD page names onset before 9 (precocious) and later than the normal range (delayed).
        - found on re-fetch: Confirmed verbatim on the NICHD page: "The physical changes that mark puberty typically begin in girls between ages 8 and 13 and in boys between ages 9 and 14." and on the same page "Precocious ... puberty begins before age 8 for girls and before age 9 for boys." StatPearls confirmed verbatim: "The onset of puberty in males ranges from 9 to 14 years of age."
        - fix: Delete `window`, leaving `timing: { typical: { from: 9, to: 14 } }` with measure "typical-range".
    - `ms-menarche-first-period` — timing.window {from: 10, to: 14} is an author-assembled band, not a range the source states, and as an "outer extent including the tails" it is false — the source's own table prints the tails it leaves out. The figures 10 and 14 do appear in the excerpt, so rule A passes literally, but they appear there as CUMULATIVE-PROBABILITY CUTPOINTS (10% by 10, 90% by 14), not as range endpoints; repurposing them as window endpoints changes what the numbers mean. Compounding it, `measure: "median"` labels the whole timing object, so the band renders under a measure label that does not describe it. The disclaimer that the window does not cover every case sits only in `population` — AUTHORING §5.1 trap 3. The author disclosed this call in their notes and named the correct fallback themselves.
        - found on re-fetch: Excerpt confirmed verbatim in NHSR 146 page 3 (extracted from https://www.cdc.gov/nchs/data/nhsr/nhsr146-508.pdf with pypdf): "Among women aged 15–44 in the United States in 2013–2017, 10% reached menarche by age 10, 53% by age 12, and 90% by age 14." Table 2 of the same report, Total row, prints the cumulative probability at each age: 0.01 (age 8), 0.04 (9), 0.10 (10), 0.26 (11), 0.53 (12), 0.77 (13), 0.90 (14), 0.96 (15). So about one in ten reached menarche at or before 10 and about one in ten after 14 — the band 10-14 is a 10th-to-90th percentile interval and excludes precisely the tails that `window` is defined to include. The median excerpt is also confirmed verbatim: "The median age at menarche decreased from 12.1 in 1995 to 11.9 in 2013–2017."
        - fix: Delete `window`, leaving `timing: { typical: { from: 11.9, to: 11.9 } }` with measure "median" — exactly the AUTHORING §5 recipe for a median and the fallback the author named. Keep the existing `population` sentence but drop its clause about the window running between the ends of the distribution, since no window will remain.
- **Advisory findings (3):**

Neither page prints a data year; both are literature summaries whose underlying reference data is decades older. schema.ts defines dataYear as "The year the DATA describes — distinct from publicationYear", and REFERENCE_FRAME_LINE renders "each figure shows its own data year", so the surface will assert a data vintage neither source states. Not one of the enumerated blocking rules and there is no truthful alternative available from these pages, but the executor should decide deliberately rather than inherit it.

    - `ms-puberty-onset-girls` — The girls and boys ranges are authored as two separate milestones rather than one record with the schema's `bySex` block, which exists for exactly this shape ({ female: Timing, male: Timing, measures, sources }). Both ranges come from one sentence in one source, so §8's "one record means one thing" arguably makes this one thing rendered twice on the spine. Not a rule violation and the author's split is defensible given the two records also draw on different StatPearls passages; flagging so the executor chooses rather than inherits.

  > All 16 source entries re-fetched and confirmed: every excerpt appears verbatim on its page, is at most 25 words, and contains the figure the record claims; publisher/publicationYear/dataYear/measures match. No URL 404s, redirects, or is blocked (the two Census PDFs were opaque to WebFetch but WebFetch saved them locally and pdftotext recovered exact prose). Rule A holds for every record: every number in every `timing` block appears in an excerpt of that record's own sources. Rules D/E/F/G/H/I/J/K/L/M/N/O all pass, independently confirmed by running the project compiler (tools/timeline-build-content.mjs via tsx), which accepts the batch. Four blocking defects are judgment-level, all under Rule B (claim exceeds or contradicts its source): a window that spans the band its own source calls lowest; a label whose plural comparison is factually false for one of the bands that follow; a `typical` range asserting a wealth peak the Census brief never prints; and a "substantial number" characterization of a projected figure the project's own band table calls "some". Six advisory items follow.

- **BLOCKING findings (4) — all repaired before listing:**
    - `ms-earnings-highest-bands` — The window contradicts the record's own source. The label is "The years in which median weekly earnings for full-time workers sit highest", but timing.window is {from: 16, to: 54}. Its own source src-bls-wkyeng-2025q2-youngest says the opposite about the low end: "Men and women ages 16 to 24 had the lowest median weekly earnings, $797 and $712, respectively." schema.ts documents `window` as "the outer extent, including the tails" of the thing the label names, so this asserts that the highest-earning years may begin at 16 — a claim the cited release explicitly refutes. AUTHORING.md §5.1 trap 3: the label is what renders, and the source must support exactly what it asserts.
        - found on re-fetch: BLS Usual Weekly Earnings, Second Quarter 2025 (released July 22, 2025), re-fetched: "By age, usual weekly earnings were highest for men ages 35 to 54: median weekly earnings were $1,502 for men ages 35 to 44 and $1,520 for men ages 45 to 54. Among women, usual weekly earnings were also highest for workers ages 35 to 54... Men and women ages 16 to 24 had the lowest median weekly earnings, $797 and $712, respectively."
        - fix: Set window to {from: 35, to: 54}, matching the two bands the release actually names as highest for both men and women (typical is already 35-54). If a wider outer extent is wanted, source the release's 55-64 band and extend to 64; do not extend downward into the band the release calls lowest.
    - `ms-entering-paid-work` — The label asserts a plural comparison that is false for one of the bands it covers. Label: "Participation in paid work is lower in the teen and early adult years than in the bands that follow." The record cites exactly one following band (25-54, at 83.6 percent). But the same BLS article reports 19.5 percent for people age 65 and older — lower than the 55.9 percent it reports for 16- to 24-year-olds. So "the bands that follow", unqualified and plural, is contradicted by the very article both cited sources come from. The record's own whatChanges gets this right in the singular ("than of the band that follows it"), which makes the label an internal inconsistency as well as an overreach.
        - found on re-fetch: BLS, "Golden years: older Americans at work and play" (May 29, 2025), re-fetched: "In the same year, those ages 55 to 64 had a 65.9-percent participation rate, while 55.9 percent of 16- to 24-year-olds were in the labor force." and "In 2024, 19.5 percent of people age 65 and older participated in the labor force".
        - fix: Make the label singular and name the comparison the sources support, e.g. "Participation in paid work is lower in the teen and early adult years than in the prime-age band that follows". Do not generalize across all later bands unless every one of them is sourced and the comparison actually holds.
    - `ms-household-wealth-by-age` — timing.typical is {from: 70, to: 74} with measure "typical-range", which encodes 70-74 as the dense zone of the arc the label names — in practice, the peak. The Census brief never says 70-74 is the highest band. I extracted the full prose of p70br-202.pdf locally: the brief names 70-74 only as a comparator against the overall median and against the 75-and-over band, and the word "highest" never appears next to an age band anywhere in the document. Encoding the peak is an inference the source does not print (AUTHORING.md §5.1 trap 4). The author's own handoff note concedes the band boundary is not robust: the SCF would place the top band at 65-74, i.e. the figure is an artifact of which survey's bands you cite.
        - found on re-fetch: U.S. Census Bureau, Wealth of Households: 2022 (P70BR-202, November 2024), full text extracted locally: "The data suggest that households generally accumulate more wealth as they age. For households in which the householder was under the age of 35, median wealth was $31,110 in 2022. For households in which the householder was between 70 and 74 years old, median household wealth was $403,000, or about 2.3 times the value of overall median wealth ($176,500). Figure 2 also suggests that aging households eventually draw down their wealth. For households in which the householder was at least 75 years old, median household wealth was $307,900, or about 80 percent of the median household wealth for householders between 70 and 74 years old." No sentence in the brief identifies a highest band.
        - fix: Either find and quote a sentence that names the highest-wealth age band, or drop timing.typical entirely and carry window only, with population stating plainly that the brief names no peak band and that band boundaries differ between the SIPP and the SCF. Do not let the record's densest rendered zone assert something no excerpt says.
    - `ms-leaving-paid-work` — whatChanges line 3 reads "A substantial number of people are still in the labor force in the oldest band the agency reports separately." Two problems. (1) The only figure this record carries for that band is 10.8 percent, and it is a 2019 projection for 2026, not an observation — yet the sentence asserts it in the present indicative as an existing fact. (2) "A substantial number" is a proportion claim worded outside the project's published band table. BAND_TABLE in schema.ts maps 0.108 to "some" (>= 0.05, < 0.20); AUTHORING.md §4 requires surface prose to carry a band from that table, not an ad-hoc adjective. On the alternative reading of "oldest band" as 65-and-over, the figure is 19.5 percent, which is also "some" under the table — so the inflation holds either way.
        - found on re-fetch: BLS TED, May 29, 2019, re-fetched: "For workers age 75 and older, the participation rate in 2026 is projected to be 10.8 percent, compared with 4.7 percent in 1996." schema.ts BAND_TABLE: { most: 0.66, aboutHalf: 0.45, many: 0.2, some: 0.05, few: 0 }.
        - fix: Rewrite using the published band vocabulary and mark the vintage, e.g. "Some people are still counted in the labor force in the oldest bands the agency reports, and the figure for the very oldest band is a projection rather than an observation." Do not use an adjective outside most/about half/many/some/few for a sourced proportion.
- **Advisory findings (6):**
    - `ms-leaving-paid-work` — The window's upper half rests on a 2019-vintage projection, and the disclosure lives only in Source.measures, not in the record's own population field. Worse for the reader: both TED source entries carry dataYear 2026, so the evidence drawer will show "data year 2026" next to a figure published in 2019 and never observed — under REFERENCE_FRAME_LINE ("each figure shows its own data year") that reads as current data. Separately, window.to is 75, but 75 is the FLOOR of an open-ended band ("age 75 and older"), used here as a ceiling, and whatChanges says participation "keeps falling in the oldest ones" — i.e. beyond where the window stops.
    - `ms-homeownership-rate-climb` — The record's central claim — that the rate climbs — is not carried by its excerpt. The cited sentence gives only the maximum (65 and over, 78.6 percent) and the minimum (under 35), so "climbing through the bands between" in whatChanges rests on Table 6, which is not quoted. The claim is true of the document (Table 6, Q2 2026: 35.2 / 60.9 / 69.7 / 75.1 / 78.6, monotone), so this is a citation gap, not a false claim. Note also that the two timing numbers appear in the excerpt as a ceiling ("under 35 years of age") and a floor ("65 years and over") but are used as the window's floor and ceiling.
    - `ms-household-wealth-by-age` — timing.window is {35, 75}, but the label promises wealth is "drawn down in the later years" and the drawdown band the brief actually names is 75-and-over — which sits at or past the window's edge. Both window endpoints also invert their excerpts' sense: 35 comes from "under the age of 35" (a ceiling used as a floor) and 75 from "at least 75 years old" (a floor used as a ceiling).
    - `ms-first-home-purchase-age` — whatChanges line 2 says the median "has moved a long way later over the decades the association has run the survey". Neither cited excerpt supports the decades-long trend; they support only "all-time high" for the current year. Separately, window is {40, 40}: AUTHORING.md §5 prescribes typical {m, m} for a median, but says nothing licensing a zero-width WINDOW, and window is documented as "the outer extent, including the tails" — first-time buyer age has a very wide spread.
    - `src-nar-first-time-buyer-share` — The excerpt "First-time home buyers made up 21% of all home buyers; their median age was 40." is verbatim on the page, but it is an infographic caption rather than body prose, and it duplicates the figure (40) already carried by src-nar-first-time-buyer-age. The page's body sentence is a stronger citation and carries both figures at once.
    - `ms-earnings-highest-bands` — measure is "median" but timing.typical is a range {35, 54}, not a point. AUTHORING.md §5 prescribes measure "median" with typical {m, m}. Here "median" describes the earnings statistic, not the age, so MEASURE_LABEL["median"] will render the word "median" beside an age range and read as a median AGE — which is not what the source measured.

"Some" is the BAND_TABLE band for both readings of the claim (0.108 and 0.195 both fall in [0.05, 0.20)), and the projection vintage is now stated on the surface. No digits, thirty-one words, within the forty-word cap.
