  > Seven sourced records plus one researchRequired. Sourced: prime working years (25-54, BLS MLR definition + BLS Beyond the Numbers highest participation rate); participation lower in the teen/early-adult band (16-25); median weekly earnings lowest in the youngest band and highest across the two middle bands (window 16-54, typical 35-54, BLS Usual Weekly Earnings 2025 Q2); participation falling away in later life (window 55-75, typical 65-74); homeownership rate climbing by age of householder (35-65, Census HVS 2026 Q2); median age of first-time home buyers (median-only point 40, NAR, reputable-secondary with its own survey named); household wealth building and later drawn down (window 35-75, typical 70-74, Census SIPP Wealth of Households: 2022).

Every candidate I found was a participation rate inside a fixed age band, which is exactly the Phase 1 age-universe trap; I refused to convert it into an age at entry. The separate sourced record I did write claims only what the source claims - that the rate is lower in one band than in the next - and its population field says so explicitly. (2) The Federal Reserve SCF was NOT used. The SCF bulletin names 65-74 as the top median-net-worth band, but that figure exists only as a table row, never as a quotable sentence, and the PDF is unparseable to the fetcher. I used the Census SIPP brief instead, which states the same shape in prose. NOTE FOR THE VERIFIER: SIPP names the early seventies (70-74) where SCF's bands would put it at 65-74; the two are consistent in shape but not in band boundaries, and my record cites only SIPP. The wealth label deliberately does not assert "peaks at" - it says wealth builds then is drawn down, which is what the brief actually says.

The upper bound of the retirement window (75) rests on a 2019 BLS TED article reporting PROJECTED participation rates for 2026, not observed ones; measures says so on both source entries. The 2024-observed source only gives an open-ended "65 and older" band. The Census HVS figure is Q2 2026, cited at a stable quarter-specific URL rather than currenthvspress.pdf, which rotates each quarter and would break verification. NAR is the only non-.gov source; it is the primary for its own survey and is marked reputable-secondary.

### `p3-analyses-family-body`

  > Four blocking findings across 8 records / 48 branches, plus four advisories. The mechanical passes are genuinely clean — I verified this with a script that reuses the project's own CRISIS_TIER/LOSS_TIER lists (content/exclusions.ts) and the sixteen NORMATIVE_PATTERNS (content/timeline/normative-lint.ts), and I poison-tested the script to confirm it fires on planted violations. Zero digits, zero percent signs, zero second person, zero normative-lint hits, zero crisis terms, loss-tier terms confined to ms-death-of-a-spouse-later-life (sensitivity "dying", permitted), every costs array accompanied by a non-empty routes array, and all eight optional records carry a "never" branch. `npx tsx tools/timeline-build-content.mjs` compiles green. The blocking findings are two id-resolution failures against the manifest, one recommendation/tone defect on the widowhood record, and one evidence-label defect on the fertility record. Note on check M: not applicable — this is not the inner-life batch — but I read the two cited ASRM source excerpts anyway, which is where finding 4 came from.

- **BLOCKING findings (4) — all repaired before listing:**
    - `ms-death-of-a-spouse-later-life` — Check A (recommendation) and check K (tone on a [SENSITIVE: dying] record). The `alternative` branch's second route ends: "A health care proxy and a durable power of attorney give a partner standing before the fact, which is when it matters most." The clause "which is when it matters most" is not a description of what tends to follow — it is an evaluative claim that one timing (before the death) is better than another, which is exactly the advice shape check A forbids. Under check K it is worse: the `alternative` branch is the one an unmarried long-term partner reads, and the record is about a partner who has died. A person who did not put those documents in place reads a sentence telling them the moment that mattered has already passed — a penalty and a verdict. It also directly contradicts the route immediately above it in the same array, which says "any of them can be put in place at any point."

### `p3-analyses-launch-learning`

- **Authored:** 8 record(s).
- **Verifier verdict:** `repairs-required`.

  > Eight records, 46 branches. Every mechanical wall passes: no digits in prose, no hits on the §5.1 normative pattern set, no crisis-tier or loss-tier terms from content/exclusions.ts, no second-person address, no percentage or rate, every costs branch carries routes, every optional:true record carries never (the two without never are optional:false in the p1 batches, so §5.7 does not require one), and every id in analyses and affectsLater resolves against the full batch corpus with no duplicate patch. Both evidence-informed labels are backed by the records' own stored excerpts ("almost 90% ... by age 30" for the departures claim; the B&B age-at-completion table for the bachelor's claim), and neither carries the figure into prose. One blocking defect: the voting record's early branch names a preferred action and a preferred moment and ranks it as easiest, which is a recommendation wearing descriptive clothing. Five advisory notes follow it, none of which block. Two structural observations worth the author knowing: the patch sets major:true on ms-completing-twelfth-grade and ms-enrolling-in-college, both of which carry no sources at all in p1-institutional-education.json, so two full milestone pages will render off unsourced records; and the compiler applies patch.major and patch.affectsLater onto the target record, so those fields are live rather than decorative.

- **BLOCKING findings (1) — all repaired before listing:**
    - `ms-voting-federal-elections` — The early branch's tends field recommends rather than describes. "Registering through a school, a licensing office or a registration drive while still at one settled address is the mechanism that matters" names a preferred action and a preferred moment, and "a first registration made from a stable address is the easiest one anyone ever files" is a superlative that ranks one timing above the others. Together they read as a tip: register early, from a settled address. Rule A covers exactly this shape — implying one timing is better as advice rather than describing what tends to follow. The branch carries no costs and no routes, so the sentence is the whole content of the branch and there is nothing beside it doing descriptive work. Compare the same author's enrolling-in-college window branch, which gets the permitted form right: "What follows is not a different education but a lower coordination cost."
        - found on re-fetch: ms-voting-federal-elections.analysis.early.tends: "Registering through a school, a licensing office or a registration drive while still at one settled address is the mechanism that matters: registration is address-bound, and a first registration made from a stable address is the easiest one anyone ever files."
        - fix: Recast as association and drop both the prescription and the superlative. E.g. "Registration is address-bound, so a registration filed from an address that does not change soon after tends to need fewer later updates than one filed from an address in flux. Schools, licensing offices and registration drives are where a large part of first registrations are made, because each is a moment when the paperwork is already open." This states the mechanism and what tends to follow without telling anyone when to file.
- **Advisory findings (5):**
    - `ms-completing-twelfth-grade` — The window branch says "The path is smoothest here," a superlative that ranks the window timing against the early and late branches in the site's own voice. It lands on the descriptive side of rule A only because the same sentence supplies the causal reason and explicitly refuses the credential-quality reading ("because everything else is scheduled to meet it, not because the credential is different"). A reader who finished at twenty-four still hears "yours was rougher." The parallel window branches on enrolling-in-college ("a lower coordination cost") and bachelors ("arranged rather than improvised") make the same point without a superlative, so the batch is internally inconsistent on this.
    - `ms-completing-twelfth-grade` — One route in the late branch is not a thing a person can go to. "Federal and state adult education funding" is a funding stream, not a service with a door — it is the reason the three routes above it are free, not a fourth route. Rule D asks that a route name something a person could actually go to or do. The branch is not at risk on rule B (three genuine routes precede it), so this is a quality note rather than a wall.
    - `ms-age-of-majority` — The interrupted branch is the one place in the batch that attaches a timing instruction to an action addressed to the reader. The tends field says enrolment in transition programs "generally has to happen before that date rather than after," and the fourth route then instructs "before the transition date." Both are statements of programme eligibility rules rather than life-timing advice, which is why this is not blocking — but the route is phrased as an instruction with a deadline attached, and it is the only route in forty-six branches that carries one.
    - `ms-voting-federal-elections` — "Too late" appears in the interrupted branch's cost. It survives the wall because it describes an administrative fact inside one election cycle, not a verdict on a life, and rule E's list does not carry it. But it is the phrase the whole lint exists to keep out of the reader's ear, and the same fact reads cleanly without it.
    - `ms-voting-federal-elections` — A route bundles two programmes under one owner and misattributes one of them. The Federal Write-In Absentee Ballot is administered by the Federal Voting Assistance Program under the Department of Defense, not by the State Department. State does run voting assistance for citizens abroad through its consular posts, so the sentence is not wrong about that half — but a reader following the route as written will look for the FWAB in the wrong place. Low confidence that this matters to the compiler; it is a route-accuracy note.
- **Repair pass:** verdict `clean`.

No other fields touched. Re-scanned the whole batch afterward: no forbidden normative phrases, no second-person address, no digits or percentages in prose fields, no loss-tier or crisis-tier vocabulary (only \"deadline\" substrings matched \"dead\"), and every branch carrying costs also carries routes. All eight records retained; file is valid JSON.

The superlative was deleted. First sentence unchanged; no costs or routes were added, so the costs/routes pairing rule is unaffected.

I also checked the file contains no second-person "you" or "your" at all.
  > 
  > Assumptions that affected the output, flagged rather than resolved silently: (1) I treated the six branch names as a menu rather than a required set, since TimingAnalysis makes each optional and the compiler only enforces never on optional records — that is what let me drop never from the two arrival-age records. (2) Where a route names a programme that varies by state, I wrote "a number of states" or "most states" rather than a count, both to stay clear of the digits wall and because I did not fetch a state tally during this batch. (3) I did not run the compiler.

### `p3-analyses-work-money`

- **Authored:** 8 record(s).
- **Verifier verdict:** `repairs-required`.

  > Every mechanical wall passes, verified programmatically over all 185 prose strings (0 digits, 0 second person, 0 percent/rate tokens, 0 hits against the full NORMATIVE_PATTERNS list ported verbatim from content/timeline/normative-lint.ts, 0 substring hits against all 53 CRISIS_TIER and 32 LOSS_TIER terms from content/exclusions.ts). Structure is sound: all 8 ids and all 6 affectsLater targets resolve in the manifest, every branch set is the full six, no branch has costs without non-empty routes, all four optional records carry a never branch, evidence is speculative x45 / contested x3 and never "calibrated". No sentence in the batch recommends a timing (checked A explicitly against better/best/ideal/wise/too late/the window to/waiting costs — no hits), and none of the 8 records is marked [SENSITIVE] in the manifest, so K does not engage. Three blocking findings, all in branch semantics rather than lexicon: the never branch on ms-p1fr-ss-earliest-claim is not about never and frames never as a failure, contradicting §5.7 and the record's own whatChanges line; and two entries sitting in "routes" arrays are statements of fact with nothing a person could go to or do.

- **BLOCKING findings (3) — all repaired before listing:**
    - `ms-p1fr-ss-earliest-claim` — The "never" branch is not about never, and it frames never as a failure. AUTHORING §5.7/§7 (and the compiler's own failure message) require that on an optional record "the never branch exists — never is a path, not a failure." This branch instead describes deferring a claim past the delayed-credit ceiling — which is the "late" branch carried further, not never — and its entire content is closure plus an irrecoverable loss. It never names the actual never case: a person who never claims a retirement benefit on their own record (not fully insured, entitled only through a spouse's or a divorced spouse's record, or a deliberate non-claim). Every other never branch in the pool names the thing not done as ordinary and states the door's status ("Not driving is an ordinary arrangement… Licensing carries no upper age, so this door stays open"; "Not voting is common and lawful, and eligibility does not expire"). Worse, the record's own whatChanges in p1-federal-retirement.json already says the opposite of this branch: "Filing here is one option among several; many people file later, and never filing at this point is an ordinary path." The author's handoff note confirms the repurposing — the skipped ms-p1fr-ss-delayed-credit-ceiling record was folded into this slot.
        - found on re-fetch: tends: "A claim not made accrues delayed retirement credits only up to the age at which those credits stop; after that point, deferral adds nothing and unclaimed months are not recovered later. Retroactive payment reaches back only a limited number of months, so this is one of the places where the door genuinely closes rather than narrowing." costs[0]: "Months deferred past the credit ceiling add nothing to the payment and are not recoverable".
        - fix: Rewrite the branch so it is about not claiming: entitlement computed from a spouse's or divorced spouse's record rather than one's own, the fact that benefits start on a filed application and there is no automatic enrolment so an unfiled claim is a state and not an error, and that entitlement itself does not expire. Move the delayed-credit-ceiling closure into the "late" branch, which already states it ("until the age at which those credits stop"). Keep the closure honest there — §7 permits "closed" as an answer — but it cannot be the whole of the never branch.
    - `ms-leaving-paid-work` — routes[1] on the "late" branch is not a route. It names nothing a person could go to or do — it is a reassurance about how the mechanism resolves on its own, and the recomputation it describes happens automatically without any action. It sits in the routes array answering the cost "Working while receiving retirement benefits before full retirement age triggers an earnings test that withholds part of the payment", and the other two routes on that branch (age discrimination law, phased retirement) do not answer that cost, so removing it naively would leave that cost with no real recovery.
        - found on re-fetch: ms-leaving-paid-work.analysis.late.routes[1]: "Withheld amounts under the earnings test are not forfeited; the payment is recomputed upward at full retirement age"
        - fix: Move the recomputation fact into "tends", where it is a mechanism, and put a real route in its place for the earnings-test cost — the Social Security Administration's annual earnings test estimator and the provision for reporting an expected change in earnings so withholding is spread rather than applied in a block, or a claim filed at or after full retirement age, where the test does not apply at all.
    - `ms-p1fma-catch-up-contributions-open` — routes[0] on the "interrupted" branch is not a route. It is a restatement of the provision the record is about, with nothing to go to and nothing to do. It answers the cost "Unused annual contribution room is not recoverable in a later year" by repeating what the record already says.
        - found on re-fetch: ms-p1fma-catch-up-contributions-open.analysis.interrupted.routes[0]: "The raised ceiling applies to every remaining qualifying year, in the employer plan and the individual arrangement both"
        - fix: Drop it or fold it into "tends". The cost is already answered by the two real routes on the branch (spousal individual retirement arrangements opening a second ceiling; health savings account and taxable accounts absorbing amounts beyond the plan ceilings), so the branch stays compliant with routes non-empty.
- **Advisory findings (7):**
    - `ms-p1fr-ss-earliest-claim` — The "window" branch turns the claiming decision into a question about the reader's own lifespan, on a record that carries no sensitivity flag and therefore no careNote and no sensitive-segment rendering. "longevity" is not in LOSS_TIER_TERMS so the lexical lint passes, but AUTHORING §6.1 states the list is "the regression backstop, not the guarantee" and that paraphrase is caught by human review — which is this pass. A reader arriving at this page with a recent diagnosis meets an actuarial argument about average lifespan with nothing set around it.
    - `ms-leaving-paid-work` — The "never" branch uses a decline euphemism on a record with no sensitivity flag. "until the end of their working capacity" plus the cost "income that stops abruptly rather than tapering" is health-decline-adjacent content occupying the branch a reader still working in their eighties would land on. AUTHORING §6 requires health-decline material to be "matter-of-fact, practical, short. No dread, no euphemism" — and material that needs that register belongs on a flagged record.
    - `ms-p1fr-ss-earliest-claim` — Factual imprecision in the "early" branch: the retirement earnings test is applied to the beneficiary's own earnings, not to the household's. A household where a non-beneficiary spouse earns above the threshold has no withholding from the beneficiary's payment.
    - `ms-first-full-time-job` — The analysis asserts a common entry band on a record that has no timing and whose own researchNote says the band has not been sourced and cannot be derived from what exists. The record in p2-work-money.json carries researchRequired:true, status "editorial", evidence "insufficient-evidence", no timing, and the note "Labor force participation rates by age band cannot answer this, because a rate inside a fixed band is not an age at entry." The sibling batch p3-analyses-launch-learning does the same thing on ms-completing-twelfth-grade and ms-enrolling-in-college, but there the "band" is an institutional sequence (the end of a school year) that is knowable without a distributional source; here it is a claim about where most people actually sit.
    - `ms-p1fma-catch-up-contributions-open` — Two never branches make unsourced proportion claims using the vocabulary AUTHORING §4 reserves for the published band table (most / about half / many / some / few), on branches labelled "speculative" with no sources attached. "Most households contribute below the ordinary ceiling" is load-bearing — it is the reason the whole branch gives for the provision never binding. ms-prime-working-years.never carries the same shape with "A minority of adults are outside the labor force across the whole of this band", and "a minority" is not a band-table term at all.
    - `ms-household-wealth-by-age` — Two route entries are phrased as the preferable of two options rather than as a named mechanism. Neither is a timing recommendation, so neither trips check A, but they are the closest the batch comes and they read as financial advice on a page whose voice is otherwise purely descriptive. The parallel case is ms-first-home-purchase-age.late.routes[0], "Shorter loan terms and larger down payments, which compress the amortization span", which instructs on how to structure a purchase rather than naming something to go to.
    - `ms-p1fma-catch-up-contributions-open` — affectsLater direction. The renderer prints these under the heading "What this touches later" (app/timeline/[id]/page.tsx line 294), but two of the six edges point at a milestone that anchors earlier on the spine: this record's anchor is fifty (timing.exact 50) and ms-household-wealth-by-age anchors at thirty-five (timing.window.from 35, no typical or exact, per the anchorAge computation in the same file). ms-earnings-highest-bands, anchored at thirty-five, points at the same target for no movement at all. Separately, DECISIONS.md [2026-09-03] records that "affectsLater is wired by the executor, not by authoring agents. Batch agents were told to omit it" — the sibling p3 batch also carries edges, so that decision may have been superseded for phase 3, but it is not recorded as such.
- **Repair pass:** verdict `clean`.

  > All three blocking findings repaired in place at F:/Programming/The Guidebook to Life/tgtl-claude-5.0/content/timeline/batches/p3-analyses-work-money.json. The "never" branch on ms-p1fr-ss-earliest-claim was rewritten to be about not claiming on one's own record (no automatic enrolment, entitlement through a spouse's or divorced spouse's record, insured status that does not lapse), and the delayed-credit-ceiling closure it had been carrying was moved into the "late" branch where the credit rule already lives. The non-route on ms-leaving-paid-work's "late" branch was moved into "tends" as a mechanism and replaced with two real routes for the earnings-test cost. The restated-provision non-route on ms-p1fma-catch-up-contributions-open's "interrupted" branch was dropped; the two remaining real routes still answer the cost. File re-parses as valid JSON; a sweep over every string confirms no normative phrases, no digits in prose, no loss-tier words on non-sensitive records, no rates or percentages, no second-person address, and no branch carrying costs without routes.

    - `ms-p1fr-ss-earliest-claim` — Rewrote the branch so it is about not claiming: benefits are paid only on a filed application with no automatic enrolment, so an unfiled claim is a state rather than a missed step; some people never become fully insured on their own covered earnings and are entitled through a spouse's or a divorced spouse's record; insured status once earned does not lapse, so the door to filing does not close. The single remaining cost (no payment for months before an application, limited retroactive reach) is answered by three real routes: filing later online or at a Social Security field office with the limited retroactive request available after full retirement age; claiming on a current or former spouse's record; and the program's benefit statement and online account showing insured status and the amount at each claiming age.
    - `ms-p1fr-ss-earliest-claim` — Moved it into the "late" branch, which already reached "until the age at which those credits stop". Added a third sentence to tends stating that past the ceiling age the credit rule adds nothing further and months deferred beyond it are not recovered, so that gain closes rather than narrows, plus a matching cost entry. The branch's existing retroactive-payment route already sits alongside it, so routes remain non-empty and partially answer the cost — stated as partial, not as full recovery.
    - `ms-leaving-paid-work` — Moved the recomputation into "tends" as a third sentence, where it reads as mechanism. Put two real routes in its place: the Social Security Administration's retirement earnings test calculator together with its procedure for reporting expected earnings so withholding is spread across the year rather than applied in a block, and a claim filed at or after full retirement age, to which the test does not apply. The branch now carries four routes, two of which answer the earnings-test cost directly.
    - `ms-p1fma-catch-up-contributions-open` — Dropped it. The substance it carried is already in the branch's tends ("What the provision does is raise the ceiling in the years that remain, not restore the years that were missed"), so no fold-in was needed. The two remaining routes — spousal individual retirement arrangements opening a second ceiling, and health savings account and taxable balances absorbing amounts beyond the plan ceilings — are real and answer the unused-contribution-room cost, so the branch stays compliant.
- **Deliberately not marked major:**
    - ms-p1fr-ss-survivor-earliest-claim — sensitivity 'dying'. A timing analysis of a route that opens through a spouse's death cannot be written as early/late/never without the branches reading as commentary on when someone was widowed. Not marked major.
    - ms-p1fma-required-minimum-distributions-begin and ms-required-minimum-distributions — the age is a compliance date, not a choice with an early/late branch; every honest 'late' branch is about the excise consequence of missing a deadline, which reads as tax advice rather than mechanism. Left to the existing legal-threshold record.
    - ms-p1fr-ss-full-retirement-age — the claiming mechanism (permanent reduction before it, delayed credits after) is fully carried by the analysis on ms-p1fr-ss-earliest-claim; duplicating it on the reference age would put the same tradeoff on the spine twice and make it look like a recommended date.
    - ms-work-in-later-life and ms-leaving-paid-work overlap; analysed only ms-leaving-paid-work so that continuing work appears as branches ('late', 'never') of one record rather than as a second record implying two separate norms.
    - ms-heard-retirement-timing, ms-heard-made-it-by-forty, ms-heard-too-late-to-switch, ms-heard-savings-rules-of-thumb — cultural-expectation records. Their content is quotation; a timing analysis would treat a saying as if it had a correct time.
    - ms-p1fr-ss-delayed-credit-ceiling — the closing of delayed credits is stated inside the 'never' branch of ms-p1fr-ss-earliest-claim, where it belongs beside the claim it constrains; as its own major record it is an age with nothing to choose about it.
    - ms-flsa-minimum-working-age, ms-flsa-hour-limits-lift, ms-flsa-hazardous-occupations-age — floors on child labor. An early/late/never analysis of a protective minimum would frame a legal floor as a timing decision a young person makes.
    - ms-p1fma-early-distribution-tax-ends and ms-p1fma-hsa-additional-contribution — mechanisms referenced as routes inside other records' branches; separate analyses would multiply near-identical account-rule reasoning across the spine.
- **Author's notes to the executor:**

  > Wrote F:/Programming/The Guidebook to Life/tgtl-claude-5.0/content/timeline/batches/p3-analyses-work-money.json — 8 records, all 6 branches each (48 branches, 185 prose fields). All ids verified against the manifest.
  > 
  > Design decisions:
  > - Every branch is a mechanism, not a verdict: compounding span, amortization order, vesting and forfeiture, tenure reset, salary anchoring, sequence risk, the permanent reduction/delayed-credit adjustment, annual ceilings that do not carry forward. Nowhere does the text say a timing is better; where a door genuinely closes (delayed credits stopping, unused contribution room, retroactive payment limits) it says so plainly rather than softening it.
  > - Every branch carrying "costs" carries at least one "routes" entry, checked programmatically. Routes name real mechanisms only: COBRA, marketplace coverage with income-based assistance, salary-history bans, returnships, state auto-IRA programs (Oregon/California/Illinois), the Saver's Credit, HUD-approved housing counseling, loan modification and forbearance, withdrawal of a Social Security application within the first year, voluntary suspension at full retirement age, spousal and divorced-spouse routes, phased retirement, SCSEP, community land trusts, home equity conversion mortgages, SBA succession resources.
  > - Optional records in the batch (first full-time job, first home purchase, catch-up contributions, earliest SS claim) each have a substantive "never" branch that does the work rather than restating the renderer's standing line. The catch-up "never" says the raised ceiling only binds on someone already pressing against the ordinary one, so for most households it is irrelevant rather than missed; the homeownership "never" describes lifelong renting as a coherent structure with liquid assets, and labels the rent-versus-buy wealth comparison "contested" because it is.
  > - "contested" used three times only, where the literature genuinely disagrees: the rent/buy accumulation comparison, the actuarial neutrality of the claiming adjustment, and the survey visibility of non-financial wealth. Everything else is "speculative" — I did not read the records' existing sources, so no branch claims "evidence-informed".
  > 
  > affectsLater used on 5 records, 6 edges total, only where one genuinely shifts another's later timing: first full-time job → household wealth, first home purchase; prime working years → peak earnings; peak earnings → household wealth; leaving paid work → earliest SS claim, household wealth; catch-up contributions → household wealth.
  > 
  > Wall compliance verified by script over all 185 prose strings: zero digits (401(k) and similar avoided entirely — written as "employer plans"); zero occurrences of any normative-list phrase or its variants, and no second person; zero rates or percent signs and no use of the word "rate"; zero crisis-tier content; zero loss-tier terms (two substring false positives, "stu-dying" and "dead-line", were rewritten rather than left to risk the lint). Normalized to American English per AUTHORING §2 — the brief's "programmes"/"re-entry programmes" phrasing became "programs".
  > 
  > Assumption worth flagging: I treated "early"/"late" on the two legal-threshold records (catch-up contributions, earliest SS claim) as taking up the provision sooner or later after it opens, since a statutory age cannot itself happen early. If the compiler or renderer expects those branches to mean something else on a legal-threshold record, those two need re-framing.
  > 
  > Did not run the compiler, per instruction.

### `p3-inner-life-topup`

- **Authored:** 4 record(s).
- **Verifier verdict:** `clean`.

  > Four records, seven sources. Zero blocking findings after a full pass over criteria A–M, backed by scripted sweeps and by a browser-level re-fetch of all three source URLs (curl gets a 403 from bls.gov and WebFetch's markdown conversion normalizes punctuation, so I read document.body.innerText directly to get exact characters).
  > 
  > VERBATIM RE-FETCH (criterion M) — all seven excerpts confirmed character for character against the live pages, including whitespace-collapsed line wraps:
  > - atus.nr0.htm literally reads "Individuals ages 15 to 19 spent 1.5 hours playing games or using a computer for leisure on an average day in 2025, more than any other age group." I specifically pressure-tested the punctuation here: the same release uses a double hyphen ("per day--more than any other age group") in the two leisure sentences the p2 batch already ships, so a comma looked like a possible verbatim-fidelity slip. It is not — the page really does use a comma in this sentence. Both fragments are contiguous and exact.
  > - The relaxing/thinking TED article literally reads "People between the ages of 55 and 64 spent about 40 minutes a day socializing and communicating and about 22 minutes a day relaxing and thinking. Those 65 and over spent about 34 minutes a day on each of these activities, while those between the ages of 25 and 34 spent about 15 minutes a day relaxing and thinking in 2025." All three excerpts are exact contiguous fragments. Published July 22, 2026; data year 2025. Both match the source records.
  > - The Read Across America article's two excerpts are exact, em dashes included. Published March 02, 2026; data year 2024. Its chart data (0.13 at 20–24 up to 0.77 at 75+, "Data ranges from 0.13 to 0.77") independently confirms that 20–24 is the lowest band and 75+ the highest, so the label's superlatives hold.
  > 
  > TIMING FIGURES — every age in a timing field appears in one of that record's own excerpts: 15/19 in "ages 15 to 19"; 25 in "ages of 25 and 34" and 65 in "Those 65 and over"; 20 in "20- to 24-year-olds" and 75 in "those 75 and older". typical sits inside window on all three sourced records (15–19 in 15–19; 65–65 in 25–65; 75–75 in 20–75).
  > 
  > CRITERIA A–L: (A) no sentence in the batch tells a reader when to do anything or ranks one timing over another; the whatChanges lines are all population descriptions. (B/C/D) no record carries an analysis, so there are no branches, no costs and no routes — and AUTHORING.md §7 plus the compiler's own rule scope the never-branch requirement to optional records that HAVE an analysis, which none of these do. This matches every p1/p2 batch; analyses arrive in the separate p3-analyses-* batches. (E) I ran all sixteen NORMATIVE_PATTERNS from content/timeline/normative-lint.ts over every string in the file: zero hits. (F) zero digits in every milestone prose field (label, population, measures, windowInWords, researchNote, notes, whatChanges) and in every source measures/notes field. Source titles do carry years, but that is bibliographic and there are 85 such titles already shipping across the existing batches. (G/H) zero crisis-tier and zero loss-tier terms anywhere, checked against the full CRISIS_TIER and LOSS_TIER lists in content/exclusions.ts. (I) no percentage or rate in any field; the excerpt figures are means in hours and minutes, which live in the one place the model permits numbers. (J) no "you"/"your" anywhere. (K) none of the four is sensitive and none should be — games, relaxing, reading and organizational membership touch no body, no diagnosis and no loss. (L) no analyses and no affectsLater fields exist, so nothing to dangle.
  > 
  > SCHEMA/COMPILER SHAPE: I could not execute tools/timeline-build-content.mjs (this Node build cannot import the .ts lint module), so I replicated its checks by hand. All pass: ids match the required patterns and collide with nothing in the other nineteen batches; no unknown milestone or source keys; excerpt word counts 14–25 against the cap of 25; whatChanges at most three lines and at most 31 words against the cap of 40; readRef "/topics" resolves in content/routes.ts (the live inventory, not the legacy JSON); retrievedOn 2026-09-04 is inside the 2026-09-03..2026-09-30 build window; the researchRequired record correctly carries no timing, no sources, both researchNote and windowInWords, and evidence "insufficient-evidence"; every source referenced exists and none is orphaned.
  > 
  > Four advisory items are listed below, none of which I would hold the batch for. The nearest thing to a blocker is the antecedent chain on the relaxing record, which I judged to clear the letter and the spirit of the rules because the record deliberately carries the antecedent sentence as its own source.
  > 
  > One correction to the author's own note for the record: the author writes that this takes inner-life "past the eight-per-lane budget". There is no per-lane cap. tools/timeline-coverage.mjs prints "per lane (§7.3 floor is eight each)" — eight is a FLOOR the lane was under at six, and this batch clears it at ten. Nothing was exceeded.

- **Blocking findings:** none.
- **Advisory findings (4):**
    - `ms-relaxing-and-thinking-time-by-age` — The record's headline figure for the oldest band is not stated in any single sentence of the source. The page says "Those 65 and over spent about 34 minutes a day on each of these activities" — "these activities" resolves only against the PREVIOUS sentence, which names socializing and communicating and relaxing and thinking. So the claim "65+ spend about 34 minutes relaxing and thinking" is a two-sentence inference, and AUTHORING.md §4.1 explicitly names "two sentences stitched together" as a thing an excerpt may not be. The risk is sharpened by the same page using the same number for a different thing two sentences earlier: "people between 15 and 24 spent about 34 minutes on those activities", where those activities are sports, exercise and recreation.
    - `ms-games-and-computer-leisure-by-age` — The label says "highest in the late teens" but the band it renders is 15–19, which starts in the mid-teens. The record's own population field says the survey's "age universe begins in the mid-teens", so the label and the field one line below it use different words for the same band. AUTHORING.md §5.1 trap 3 warns that a hedge in population does not rescue a label; here the label is not overclaiming the superlative (the source does say "more than any other age group"), only glossing the band imprecisely.
    - `ms-games-and-computer-leisure-by-age` — The two sources for this record are overlapping fragments of one 28-word sentence, split because the full sentence exceeds the 25-word excerpt cap. Together they reconstruct the entire sentence, which means the split functions as a route around the cap rather than as two independent pieces of evidence. The second source's excerpt also contains no figure the record claims — its only digit is the year — where the schema comment says an excerpt "CONTAINS the figure the record claims".
    - `ms-belonging-to-groups-and-organizations-by-age` — windowInWords asserts a direction — belonging "is often described as more common in the later adult years than in the earliest" — attributed to nobody. Its sibling researchRequired record in the same lane, ms-religious-identity-and-attendance-by-age, states its shape against a named national survey and then says exactly why that survey cannot carry it. This one appeals to general impression, which is the register the cardinal rule exists to keep out, even though no number is asserted and the clause immediately disclaims itself.
- **Deliberately not marked major:**
    - Voter turnout by age — NOT authored. The pool already carries ms-voter-turnout-highest-in-later-life (civic-legal), sourced from the same Census CPS Voting and Registration figures. Re-authoring it into inner-life would be the same measurement counted twice on the spine.
    - Volunteering through an organisation by age — NOT authored. Already in the pool as ms-formal-volunteering-common-ages (civic-legal), sourced from the Census/AmeriCorps Civic Engagement and Volunteering Supplement. Duplicate.
    - Making a will or an advance directive — NOT authored. Already in the pool as ms-will-and-advance-directive-by-age (civic-legal, sensitivity dying), sourced from the Pew aging report. Duplicate, and it is a sensitive record that already carries its careNote and its two required routes.
    - Religious attendance or affiliation changing with age — NOT authored as a new record. ms-religious-identity-and-attendance-by-age already exists in inner-life and is deliberately marked researchRequired because the available Pew material compares two age bands at one moment and the same report says cohorts have not become more religious as they aged. I fetched the Pew attendance and congregational involvement report in this pass; its narrative says only that older Americans are more likely than younger Americans to participate, with no age band beside a figure in sentence form. That confirms the existing researchNote rather than resolving it, so I left the record as it stands.
    - Time spent alone by age — considered and dropped. The Pew short read that carries the age breakdown refused an automated fetch, and the BLS Economics Daily piece on time alone breaks the figures out by household type rather than by age band, so no verbatim age-plus-figure sentence was obtainable.
    - Arts attendance by age (NEA Survey of Public Participation in the Arts) — considered and dropped. The published narrative discusses change since the prior wave rather than naming the age band with the highest attendance, so no clean window could be sourced.

  > Wrote F:/Programming/The Guidebook to Life/tgtl-claude-5.0/content/timeline/batches/p3-inner-life-topup.json with four milestones and seven sources. Three are fully sourced statistical-norm records, one is honestly marked researchRequired. This takes the Inner life & meaning lane from six to ten records, past the eight-per-lane budget.

The sentence is twenty-eight words, over the excerpt cap, so it is carried as two contiguous verbatim fragments of the same sentence under two source ids: the first holds the age band and the hours, the second holds the comparative tail. I re-fetched this page twice with a targeted prompt and got the same sentence back both times.
  > 2. ms-relaxing-and-thinking-time-by-age — window 25–65, typical 65–65, measure mean. BLS Economics Daily, "Americans age 15 and over read for an average of 16 minutes per day in 2025" (published 2026, ATUS 2025 data). Three excerpts, one per age band. Note the ordering claim rests on a sentence whose antecedent ("each of these activities") lives in the preceding sentence, which is why the midlife excerpt is carried as its own source — a verifier re-fetching will need both to see the claim stand.
  > 3. ms-reading-for-personal-interest-by-age — window 20–75, typical 75–75, measure mean. BLS Economics Daily, "It's Read Across America Day" (published 2026-03-02, ATUS 2024 data). The age-gap sentence was confirmed on a second fetch that asked for the complete sentence from capital letter to period.

AmeriCorps returned HTTP 403 to the fetch tool, the Census CEV story names only generations and a virtual-volunteering split, and Pew's congregational involvement narrative gives no age band beside a figure. No number was asserted.

  > VERIFICATION RUN: a script checked the emitted JSON for digits in every prose field (label, population, measures, windowInWords, researchNote, notes, whatChanges), excerpt word counts (max was twenty-five), whatChanges length and count, typical contained by window, the full normative-language list, and the loss-tier list. Zero problems.

  > ASSUMPTIONS THAT AFFECTED THE OUTPUT:

If the intent was in fact to mirror the civic records into inner-life, that call goes the other way and these three would be replaced.

## Batches authored by the executor rather than by an agent

One batch was written by the executor during the rolling human read and is recorded here
separately, because it did NOT go through the author -> adversarial-verifier -> repair pipeline
and should not be read as though it had.

### `p4-exec-child-four-years` — two records

- **Two records.** (1) The four-year developmental checklist entry, because age four was the
  single year on the timeline with no covering record. (2) Life expectancy AT SIXTY-FIVE, because
  a reader-persona pass found the later-life intro was showing only the at-BIRTH figure, and §5.4
  requires both. The at-65 figure renders as a remaining span — what the source prints — so the
  timeline performs no arithmetic.
- **Why the first:** age four was the single year on the whole timeline with no covering record. Its honest
  empty state therefore read "Nothing discrete commonly happens at 4. The windows above are still
  open" — while no window was open. One sourced record closes it.
- **How it was sourced:** the executor fetched the CDC "Learn the Signs. Act Early." four-year
  checklist directly, quoted its heading verbatim ("What most children do by 4 years"), and
  fetched the page's own last-review date separately rather than assuming it from the sibling
  pages the earlier batch used.
- **What checked it:** the content compiler, which refused the record on its first submission
  because the executor had put a digit in a prose field — the same rule that governs every agent.
  It then passed T-1, T-6, T-7 and T-11 with the rest of the pool.
- **What did NOT check it:** a separate adversarial verifier. That is the honest gap in this one
  record, and it is why it is listed apart from the twenty-two that did get one.

- `src-statpearls-tanner-females` — StatPearls Publishing, via NCBI Bookshelf (Emmanuel M, Bokor BR) · https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK470280/
- `src-statpearls-tanner-males` — StatPearls Publishing, via NCBI Bookshelf (Emmanuel M, Bokor BR) · https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK470280/
- `src-timing-natural-menopause-review` — Obstetrics and Gynecology Clinics of North America (via PubMed Central) · https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3285482/

- `src-usafacts-turnout-oldest` — USAFacts (compiled from U.S. Census Bureau Current Population Survey data, which the article names as its source) · https://usafacts.org/articles/how-many-americans-voted-in-2024/
- `src-usafacts-turnout-youngest` — USAFacts (compiled from U.S. Census Bureau Current Population Survey data, which the article names as its source) · https://usafacts.org/articles/how-many-americans-voted-in-2024/

# The fix pass — batch `p5-fixpass`, 2026-09-04

Written by hand rather than generated, because this batch was run by the executor
directly rather than by a fan-out of authoring agents. It is recorded to the same
standard: every source below was **re-fetched during the fix pass**, and the verdict
says what the re-fetch actually returned.

The batch exists because of the acceptance review's **F2** — four records that encoded
a comparison between two age bands as a window, so that a true figure about one band
rendered beside every year between the two. Re-authoring them meant re-verifying the
sources they rest on and, where the fix removed timing, replacing what it removed.

## What was re-fetched, and what came back

| Source | Re-fetch verdict, 2026-09-04 |
|---|---|
| `src-bls-ted-reading-age-gap` | **CONFIRMED.** The sentence is on the page character-for-character: *"Time spent reading varied widely across age groups, with 20- to 24-year-olds averaging 0.13 hours (8 minutes) per day, while those 75 and older averaged 0.77 hours, or 46 minutes per day."* Two bands, two figures — which is why this source now carries two records rather than one window drawn between them. |
| `src-bls-ted-reading-all-ages` | **CONFIRMED.** *"In 2024, Americans over the age of 14 spent 0.28 hours—about 17 minutes—on an average day reading for personal interest."* Data year 2024, as recorded. |
| `src-bls-atus-leisure-oldest` | **CONFIRMED.** *"On average, adults age 75 and over spent 7.4 hours engaged in leisure and sports activities per day--more than any other age group."* |
| `src-bls-atus-leisure-lowest` | **CONFIRMED.** *"Those ages 35 to 44 averaged 3.9 hours doing leisure and sports activities per day--less than all other age groups."* |
| `src-bls-ted-relaxing-early-adult` | **CONFIRMED.** *"those between the ages of 25 and 34 spent about 15 minutes a day relaxing and thinking in 2025."* |
| `src-bls-ted-relaxing-late-midlife` | **CONFIRMED.** *"People between the ages of 55 and 64 spent about 40 minutes a day socializing and communicating and about 22 minutes a day relaxing and thinking."* |
| `src-bls-ted-relaxing-oldest-band` | **CONFIRMED.** *"Those 65 and over spent about 34 minutes a day on each of these activities"*, whose antecedent is the sentence above it — which is why both sources stay on the oldest-band record. |
| `src-usafacts-turnout-oldest` | **CONFIRMED.** *"In 2024, 74.7% of voters ages 65 or older voted, the highest rate among all age groups."* |
| `src-usafacts-turnout-youngest` | **CONFIRMED.** *"Less than half of voters ages 18 to 24 voted, and were the least likely age group to vote in 2024."* |
| `src-usafacts-turnout-denominator` | **CONFIRMED.** *"It's important to note, however, that the Census Bureau only provides this data as a percent of total population, not of registered voters."* |
| `src-census-cev-volunteering-by-age` | **CONFIRMED.** *"People ages 16-17 had the highest rate of all age groups at 28%, followed by people ages 45 to 54 at 27%."* Two bands in one sentence, now two records. |
| `src-pew-aging-planning-seventy-and-older` | **CONFIRMED** by direct extraction of the PDF's own text streams: *"Most adults ages 70 and older have a will and a living will or advance directive"*. |
| `src-pew-aging-planning-seventies` | **CONFIRMED** the same way: *"Roughly two-thirds of adults in their 70s (66%) say they have created a will and 64% have a living will or advance directive."* |
| `src-pew-aging-planning-sixties` | **CONFIRMED** the same way: *"Among adults in their 60s, 46% have a will and 44% have a living will or advance directive."* |
| `src-pew-aging-planning-when-most` | **PARTIALLY CONFIRMED, recorded rather than hidden.** The clause *"until they are in their 70s."* is present verbatim in the extracted text, immediately before the seventies sentence; the opening words the excerpt also quotes sit in a font run this extraction could not decode, so the sentence could not be reassembled end to end by this pass. The record's timing does not rest on it — the window is carried by `src-pew-aging-planning-seventy-and-older`, confirmed in full above — and the source was verified by the pipeline's own verifier and again by the acceptance review, which read the PDF directly. No figure changed. |
| `src-fidelity-savings-guideline` | **WITHDRAWN.** The excerpt was true and the record that used it was not: a firm's published savings guideline names ages, and the record turned those ages into a window across most of adult life for a *saying*. The saying now carries no timing at all, so the source has no record to serve and was removed with it. |
| `src-census-carra-founder-age` | **CONFIRMED, and NEW.** Fetched as a PDF from `census.gov` and read by direct extraction of its text streams. The clause quoted — *"the mean age for the entrepreneurs at founding is 41.9"* — appears twice in the paper, once in the introduction and once under Table 2 (*"we see that the mean age at founding is 41.9"*). Authors: Pierre Azoulay (MIT and NBER), Benjamin F. Jones, J. Daniel Kim (MIT), Javier Miranda (U.S. Census Bureau), dated March 2018; data are founders of new United States firms with employees, founded 2007–2014, from the Census Bureau's Longitudinal Business Database. The paper states that its opinions and conclusions are the authors' own, and the record's `measures` says so. The same paper reports a *higher* mean for the fastest-growing ventures; this record does not claim that figure. |

## What the batch changed

- **Four records re-authored** (review F2): `ms-reading-for-personal-interest-by-age`,
  `ms-daily-leisure-time-by-age`, `ms-relaxing-and-thinking-time-by-age` and
  `ms-heard-savings-rules-of-thumb`.
- **Three more found by scanning for the same signature** and re-authored with them:
  `ms-voter-turnout-highest-in-later-life`, `ms-formal-volunteering-common-ages` and
  `ms-will-and-advance-directive-by-age`. The scan is described in `DECISIONS.md`.
- **One record authored** (`ms-founding-a-business-mean-age`), for the reason its own
  `notes` field states.

Every window in this batch is an age band the source names, and nothing wider.
